completeness for monads and theories
Roman Kositsyn

Abstract
. We generalize the correspondence between theories and monads with arities of [ BMW12 ] to ∞ \infty -categories. Additionally, we introduce the notion of complete theories that is unique to the ∞ \infty -categorical case and provide a completion construction for a certain class of theories. Along the way we also develop the necessary technical material related to the flagged bicategory of correspondences and lax functor in the ∞ \infty -categorical context.



1.
Introduction

The principal goal of this paper is to generalize the results of
[
BMW12
]
to the
\infty
-categorical case, so we start this introduction with a brief review of the objects of study and results of
[
BMW12
]
. That paper is concerned with providing an isomorphism between two apparently quite distinct categories - the category of
nervous theories
and the category of
monads with arities
. A
category with arities
is a presentable category
\mathscr{E}
together with a full subcategory
i_{\mathscr{A}}:\mathscr{A}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E}
such that the corresponding nerve functor
\nu_{\mathscr{A}}:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{A})
is fully faithful, where
\nu_{\mathscr{A}}(X)(A)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(i_{\mathscr{A}}(A),X)
. A functor
F:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
betwen categories with arities
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{A})
and
(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B})
is called
arity-respecting
if it takes
\mathscr{A}
-colimits to colimits in
\mathscr{F}
. A monad with arities is then simply a monad in the bicategory of categories and functors with arities (and arbitrary natural transformations). A nervous theory is a bijective-on-objects functor
j:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\Theta
such that
j^{*}j_{!}
restricts to an endofunctor of
\mathscr{E}\subset\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{A})
. The main result of
[
BMW12
]
provides an equivalence between the category of nervous theories and the category of monads with arities. A common source of these objects are cartesian monads on presheaf categories (studied in
[
Web04
]
for ordinary categories and in
[
CH19
]
in the
\infty
-categorical contexts) which have canonical arities, however the monad/theory correspondence extends to more general cases that include Lawvere theories
[
Law63
]
and the free groupoid monad described in the last section of
[
BMW12
]
.

We generalize
[
BMW12
]
in three principal directions. Firstly, we extend it to the general
\infty
-categorical context. This is not as straightforward as one might think - already the notion of a nervous theory presents some issues. More specifically, the problem is with the notion of a bijective-on-objects functor. The reason they appear in
[
BMW12
]
is essentially because there is an equivalence between monads in the category of correspondences with the underlying object given by a category
C
and bijective-on-objects functors
F:C\rightarrow D
. Our model of choice for
\infty
-categories are complete Segal spaces. With some care one can prove this equivalence for an arbitrary
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
(which is one of the results of
section

5
), however the corresponding Segal space
\mathscr{D}
will generally not be complete. Addressing this issue behooves us to define the category of correspondences in the setting of Segal spaces and prove some basic results about it, which we do in
section

2
and
section

3
.

Another way in which we generalize
[
BMW12
]
is our use of lax functors. Lax functors were introduced in
[
Str72
]
and they roughly correspond to functors that respect compositions and identities only up to a non-invertible 2-morphism. In particular, a lax functor from a point to a bicategory
B
is the same thing as a monad in
B
. Since monads in the bicategory of categories with arities correspond to nervous theories, one may inquire about the class of objects corresponding to lax functors from a more general category
\mathscr{C}
, and we give an answer to this question in the form of the notion of a
\mathscr{C}
-
theory
introduced in
section

6
.

Our final contribution is the notion of
completeness
of a
\mathscr{C}
-theory. This notion is unique to the
\infty
-categorical setting and it generalizes the notion of completeness of ordinary Segal spaces. We adapted it from
[
CH19
]
in which it was introduced in a rather general context of cartesian monads and algebraic patterns. Namely, the main result of
[
CH19
]
provides an equivalence between saturated algebraic patterns and complete cartesian monads on presheaf categories and also posits that the inclusion of the category of complete cartesian monads into the category of all cartesian monads admits a left adjoint called
completion
and that the categories of algebras for a monad and for its completion are isomorphic. Although The notion of completeness makes sense for a general
\mathscr{C}
-theory
\mathscr{T}
, we are only able to provide the completion construction for a rather restrictive class of theories which we call
good
(those include the theories associated to cartesian monads on presheaf categories). We now describe the contents of each section in more detail.

In
section

2
we construct the bicategory of correspondences
\mathrm{Corr}
(which are also sometimes called profunctors, distributors and bimodules). This bicategory was originally introduced in
[
Bén73
]
and numerous accounts of it have been written since. The
\infty
-categorical version appears in
[
Lur09
, Section 2.3.1.]
,
[
AF17
]
,
[
Hau19
]
and
[
Hau15
]
, our only novelty here is a consistent use of the formalism of algebraic patterns. Near the end of the section we also introduce an explicit description of the full subcategory
\mathrm{Span}
of
\mathrm{Corr}
on constant Segal spaces.

In
section

3
we prove certain basic properties of the bicategory of correspondences. All of those properties can be found for example in
[
Str80
]
for ordinary categories, however we could not find appropriate references for all of them in the
\infty
-categorical context. Our main result here is the combination of
3.7
and
3.28
that state that the bicategory of Segal spaces, functors and natural transformations
\mathrm{Cat}
as well as its opposite
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}}
can be seen as a faithful subcategory of
\mathrm{Corr}
through a functor that sends every category to itself and every functor
F
to the correspondence
F_{!}
(resp. the correspondence
F^{*}
). Another important result in this section is
3.18
that provides an isomorphism between the category of correspondences between
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
and the category of correspondences between their completions. In particular, this allows us to connect our notion of correspondences to the more classical one used in
[
Lur09
]
and
[
AF17
]
.

In
section

4
we introduce the notion of a lax functor from a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
to a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
. In defining the lax functors we follow the approach of
[
GR17
]
, namely we view
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{B}
as coCartesian fibrations over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
and define a (unital) lax functor to be a morphism
\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
of categories over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
that takes coCartesian morphisms lying over inert (resp. over inert and surjective) morphisms to coCartesian morphisms. To make working with this notion more convenient, we introduce for an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
with a subcategory
\mathscr{D}
such that
\mathscr{C}_{0}\cong\mathscr{D}_{0}
an algebraic pattern
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
whose category of Segal spaces has objects
(\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
that admit coCartesian liftings for all morphisms
f\in\mathscr{D}
and morphisms given by functors over
\mathscr{C}
that preserve those liftings. We then use this to prove that the categories of lax and unital lax functors from a given
\mathscr{C}
are corepresentable by a twofold Segal spaces
L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
and
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}\mathscr{C}
respectively. Near the end of the section we introduce the notion of lax limits and lax colimits. The classical reference for this notion is
[
Str76
]
, the notion was also introduced in
[
GHN15
]
in the special case of the bicategory
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{comp}}
and more recently in
[
GHL20a
]
for a general bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, however we make no effort to connect those definitions to ours.

Section

5
is probably the most technical part of the paper. In it we first introduce for a given Segal space
\mathscr{C}
the category
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
whose objects are morphisms
(\mathscr{D}\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E})
in
\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
such that for every
c\in\mathscr{C}
we
f_{c}
induces an isomorphism
(\mathscr{D}_{c})_{0}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathscr{E}_{c})_{0}
and such that for every morphism
f:c\rightarrow c^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}
we also have
\mathscr{D}_{f}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{E}_{f}
. Observe that in the special case
\mathscr{C}\cong*
this category is simply the category of isomorphism-on-objects functors
F:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
. The first main result of this section identifies
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
with a subcategory
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax},!}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Corr})
of the category of lax functors from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathrm{Corr}
having the same objects but only those natural transformations
\alpha
for which each component
\alpha_{c}
is a correspondence of the form
f_{c,!}
. In particular, it follows that to every lax functor
F:\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathrm{Corr}
we can attach the Segal space
\mathscr{E}
. Our second main result describes
\mathscr{E}
as a lax colimit of the functor
F
. At the end of the section we discuss the notion of completeness. We define an object
(\mathscr{D}\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{E})
of
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}

left-complete
if
\mathscr{D}
is a complete Segal space and
complete
if both
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{E}
are complete. The fact that the inclusion
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{lcomp},{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}}_{/\mathscr{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
of the full subcategory on left-complete objects admits a left adjoint is relatively elementary, however the fact that
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{comp},{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}}_{/\mathscr{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{lcomp},{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
also admits a left adjoint
L^{\mathrm{i.o.}}
is much more difficult and is a subject of
5.35
.

In the final
section

6
we introduce the main objects of interest of this paper - the category of
\mathscr{C}
-theories for a complete Segal space
\mathscr{C}
and the category of correspondences with arities
\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{art}}
. We must note here that our setup is slightly different from
[
BMW12
]
. First of all, we do not require the category
\mathscr{E}
to be presentable and instead consider an arbitrary full subcategory
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S})
. Moreover, the category of morphisms from
(\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{C}},\mathscr{C})
to
(\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{D}},\mathscr{D})
is given by a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
that take
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{C}}
to
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{D}}
. This might look different from
[
BMW12
]
, however it is in fact not: observe that if we are given a categories with arities
(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{A})
and
(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B})
in the sense of
[
BMW12
]
then any arity-respecting functor
F:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
defines a colimit preserving functor
\mathscr{A}\overset{i_{\mathscr{A}}}{\hookrightarrow}\mathscr{E}\xrightarrow{F}\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\nu_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{B})
which can be seen a a correspondence from
\mathscr{A}
to
\mathscr{B}
, and moreover the functor
F
is uniquely defined by this correspondence. We also define a
\mathscr{C}
-theory to be an object
(\mathscr{T}_{0}\xrightarrow{t}\mathscr{T}_{1})
of
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{lcomp},{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
for which each
\mathscr{T}_{0,c}
for all
c\in\mathscr{C}
is a category with arities, each correspondence
\mathscr{T}_{f}
for a morphism
f
in
\mathscr{C}
respects arities and also each monad
t^{*}_{c}t_{c,!}
respects arities. We also define the category of
models
by means of the following pullback square

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The main result of this section is
theorem

6.4
that describes an equivalence between the category of
\mathscr{C}
-theories and the category of lax functors
F:\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{art}}
and natural transformations of the form
(f_{c})_{!}
. It moreover defines an isomorphism between
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathscr{T}}(\mathscr{S})
and
\mathrm{coCone}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{art}}}(*,F_{\mathscr{T}})
. In the special case
\mathscr{C}\cong*
this reduces to the isomorphism between the algebras over a monad with arities and the category of models for the corresponding theory. Finally, we consider the notion of completeness for
\mathscr{C}
-theories. A
\mathscr{C}
-theory is called complete if the underlying object of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
is complete. Unlike in the case of algebraic patterns of
[
CH19
]
, the inclusion
\mathrm{Th}^{\mathrm{comp}}(\mathscr{C})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Th}(\mathscr{C})
does not admit a left adjoint. However
theorem

6.9
states that the inclusion
\mathrm{Th}^{\mathrm{comp}}(\mathscr{C})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Th}^{\mathrm{good}}(\mathscr{C})
does, where
\mathrm{Th}^{\mathrm{good}}(\mathscr{C})
denotes the subcategory of good
\mathscr{C}
-theories described in
6.7
. The
*
-theories attached to algebraic patterns are good, and it follows from the universal properties that our completion construction corresponds to the completion construction of
[
CH19
]
.

Notations and conventions
:

Our standard reference for
\infty
-categories is
[
Lur09
]
, however we will also use the equivalence between quasicategories and complete Segal spaces (see e.g.
[
JT07
]
) without further mention. We will typically call
\infty
-categories simply categories except in cases where it may lead to confusion. We denote by
\mathscr{S}
the category of spaces and by
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{C})
the category of presheaves on a category
\mathscr{C}
. We assume the reader is familiar with the terminology and results of
[
CH19
]
. For an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
we will typically denote
\mathrm{O}
the category
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathscr{O}}(\mathscr{S})
of Segal
\mathscr{O}
-spaces. In particular, we will denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}
the algebraic pattern of Segal spaces (i.e.
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
with the usual active/inert factorization and with elementary objects given by
[0]
and
[1]
) and by
\mathrm{Cat}
the category of Segal spaces. The category of complete Segal spaces will instead be denoted
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{comp}}
. We will use the terms ”twofold Segal” space and ”flagged bicategory” interchangeably (the latter being the terminology introduced in
[
AF18
]
).

2.
Definition of Corr

In this section we present the construction of the flagged bicategory
\mathrm{Corr}
whose objects are Segal spaces and whose morphisms are given by correspondences. Our main result is thus
2.9
which explicitly constructs (the underlying Segal space of)
\mathrm{Corr}
. We then continue to give an explicit description of the bicategory
\mathrm{Span}
which we prove in
2.17
is a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Corr}
on constant Segal spaces.

Proposition 2.1
.

Consider an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}
together with a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-space
X
which we view as a presheaf on
\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Then there is an algebraic pattern
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
which is extendable (resp. saturated) if
\mathscr{O}
is, such that Segal spaces for
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
correspond to Segal spaces for
\mathscr{O}
endowed with a morphism to X. Any morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of Segal spaces induces an extendable Segal morphism
f:\mathscr{O}_{X/}\rightarrow\mathscr{O}_{Y/}
.

Proof.

Define
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
to be a Segal fibration associated to the functor

(X:\mathscr{O}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}):i\mapsto X(I)\cong\underset{E\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{I/}}{\lim}X(E),

where the last isomorphism follows since
X
is assumed to be a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-space. It then follows from
[
CH19
, Corollary 9.17]
that it admits a structure of an extendable algebraic pattern if
\mathscr{O}
is extendable.

To prove the statement about Segal sheaves on
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
, recall from
[
AF17
, Lemma 4.1]
that for a category
\mathscr{C}
and
p\in\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{C})
we have

\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{C}_{/P})\cong\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{C})_{/P}.

Applying it to our case we see that every Segal
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
-space
F
automatically admits a morphism to
X
.

Now we need to prove that a morphism
(\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{f}X)
viewed as a presheaf on
(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{op}}
is a Segal space if and only if
\mathscr{F}
is a Segal space for
\mathscr{O}
. First, assume that
\mathscr{F}
is a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-space and fix an object
c\in\mathscr{O}
and
x\in X(c)
. Then since by definition
(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}\cong\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el}}_{c/}
we need to prove that the fiber
\mathscr{F}_{x}
of
f
over
x
is also a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-space. To do this consider the following pullback square

Since the left vertical maps is a right fibrations, we have by
[
CH19
, Corollary 7.17]
the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(i^{*}i_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})\cong\mathscr{P}((\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(i_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})\cong\mathscr{P}((\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}((\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{op}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{X,*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\mathrm{el}}_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}

where the isomorphism
i^{*}i_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el}}\cong(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el}}
follows since
i
is a fully faithful inclusion and the isomorphism
i_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}}\cong(\mathscr{O}_{/X})^{\mathrm{int}}
follows since
X
is a Segal space.

Finally, given a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
we have a natural morphism
f_{!}:\mathscr{O}_{/X}\rightarrow\mathscr{O}_{/Y}
given by sending
(h_{I}\rightarrow X)\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{O}}(h_{i},X)\cong X(I)
to
h_{I}\rightarrow X\xrightarrow{f}Y
. The fact that
f_{!}
is extendable once again follows from
[
CH19
, Proposition 9.5]
. To prove that it is Segal simply observe that the natural morphism
\mathpzc{(\mathscr{O}_{/X})}^{\mathrm{el}}_{(h_{I}\rightarrow X)/}\rightarrow\mathpzc{(\mathscr{O}_{/Y})}^{\mathrm{el}}_{f_{!}(h_{I}\rightarrow X)/}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

For a saturated pattern
\mathscr{O}
and a Segal sheaf
X
there is a natural equivalence

\mathscr{O}_{/X}\cong\underset{(o\rightarrow X)\in\mathscr{O}_{/X}}{\lim}\mathscr{O}_{/o}.

Proof.

By Yoneda lemma
X\cong\underset{(o\rightarrow X)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X}}{\mathrm{colim}}\>h_{o}
, so since
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}})
is an
\infty
-topos we see that

\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X})\cong\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}})_{/X}\cong\underset{(o\rightarrow X)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X}}{\lim}\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/o}).

Observe that since
\mathscr{O}
is saturated, every
o\in\mathscr{O}
is itself a Segal
\mathscr{O}
-space, so
\mathscr{O}_{/o}
is also an algebraic pattern. Now we need to prove that the equivalence
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X})\cong\underset{(o\rightarrow X)\in\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/X}}{\lim}\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/o})
respects the subcategory of Segal spaces. First, observe that for a Segal
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
-space
(\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{f}X)
and
(o\xrightarrow{\gamma}X)
it follows from
2.1
that the restriction
\gamma^{*}\mathscr{F}
is a Segal
\mathscr{O}_{/o}
-space. Conversely, assume that
(\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{f}X)
is such that all
\gamma^{*}\mathscr{F}
are Segal spaces. Then it follows that for every
o\in\mathscr{O}
and
x\in X(o)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{op}})}(h_{o},X)
we have that the fiber
\mathscr{F}_{x}
satisfies the Segal condition, which is exactly what we need for
\mathscr{F}
to be a Segal
\mathscr{O}_{/X}
-space.
∎

Definition 2.3
.

Call a morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
in
\Delta

cellular
if
f(i+1)\leq f(i)+1
. Denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
the full subcategory of
(\Delta_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{op}}
on cellular morphisms.

Proposition 2.4
.

\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
can be given a structure of an extendable algebraic pattern such that the inclusion
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}
is an extendable morphism of algebraic patterns. Moreover, the morphism
i^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{m,!}
restricts to a morphism

i^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{m,!}:\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}_{/[m]}

between the corresponding categories of Segal objects.

Proof.

To define the required structure we declare the subcategory of elementary objects and the subcategories of active and inert morphisms of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
to be the preimages of the respective subcategories of
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}
under
i^{\mathrm{Cell}}
. To prove that this gives
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
the structure of an algebraic pattern it suffices to prove that it is closed under the active/inert decomposition. In other words, we need to prove that if in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

both
f
and
h
are cellular, then
g
is also cellular. This follows since
i
identifies
[l]
with a subinterval of
[k]
.

To prove the extendability of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
and
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}
, we first observe that if
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
is a cellular morphism, then so are its restrictions to all elementary subintervals
i:[1]\hookrightarrow[n]
. Together with the fact that
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
is a full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}
this implies that
\mathpzc{(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})}^{\mathrm{el}}_{f/}\cong\mathpzc{(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]})}^{\mathrm{el}}_{i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}(f)/}
. Since the active/inert factorization for
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
is induced from that of
\mathpzc{Cat}
, we also see that for any active morphism
\phi:i^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{m}(f)\twoheadrightarrow g
we have
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{el}}_{/[m]}(\phi)\cong\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[m]}(\phi)
. This proves the relative Segal condition for both
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
and
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}
.

We will now prove that for every
f\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}
we have that
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}
is a Segal category. This will in particular imply that
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
is an extendable algebraic pattern. Since we know that
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}
is a Segal category it suffices to prove the following statement: given a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

in which for every interval
[i,i+1]\in[n]
we have that
g|_{\{a(i),a(i)+1,...,a(i+1)\}}
is cellular, it follows that
g
itself is cellular. This is obvious since
a
by definition preserves the endpoints.

It now remains to prove that
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}
-colimits distribute over
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{el}}_{f/}
-limits. To prove this we first make the condition more explicit. First factor
f
as
[n]\xrightarrow{f_{s}}[\overline{n}]\xrightarrow{f_{i}}[m]
where
f_{i}
is injective and
f_{s}
is surjective and let
s_{j}
be the cardinality of the fiber of
f_{s}
over
j
for
0\leq j\leq s
. Without loss of generality, we can assume
f(0)=0
and
f(n)=m
. First, denote by
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{unit}}_{/f}
the subcategory of
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}
on those active morphisms
f\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}g
whose restriction to any elementary subinterval
[s,s+1]\subset[n]
that is sent to a point by
f
is identity, we claim that it is cofinal. Indeed, using the Segal condition for
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}
proved above we see that

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}\cong\bigtimes_{(f\rightarrowtail e)\in\overline{(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{el}}}_{f/}}(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/e},

where
\overline{(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{el}}}_{f/}
denotes the full subcategory of
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{el}}_{f/}
on those morphisms
(f\rightarrowtail e)
for which
e
is an elementary morphism of the form
[1]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[n]
. Observe that for every elementary subinterval
e
that is sent to the identity in
[m]
we have

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/e}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}}_{/[1]}.

It follows that

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}\cong(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}}_{/[1]})^{\sum_{i=0}^{\overline{n}}s_{i}}\times(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{unit}}_{/f}.

Observe that
\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}}_{/[1]}
has a final object, and so the subcategory
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{unit}}_{/f}
is indeed cofinal.

It follows that we can assume that
f
is injective. Now let
[j,j+1]
be a subinterval of
[n]
, observe that

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{unit}}_{/[j,j+1]}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}\times(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{f(j+1)-f(j)-1}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty},

where
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
(resp.
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
) denotes the subcategory of
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
on morphisms that preserve the minimal (resp. maximal) element. Indeed, an object of
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{unit}}_{/[j,j+1]}
can be identified with a surjective cellular morphism to
[f(j),f(j+1)]\subset[m]
. An object like this is uniquely determined by its fibers over each
i\in[f(j),f(j+1)]
, and those fibers are all nonempty, so it can be identified with an object of
(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{f(j+1)-f(j)+1}
. An arbitrary morphism between those objects can also be identified with a morphism in
(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{f(j+1)-f(j)+1}
, observe that this morphism is active if and only if its restriction to the first component belongs to
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
and its restriction to the last component to
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
, which concludes the proof of the claim. Since both
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
and
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
have a final object we see that
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{unit}}_{/f}
has a cofinal subcategory of the form
(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{m-n}
.

Now we will need to introduce some notation. Let
\mathscr{F}
be a Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
-space, denote by
\mathscr{F}_{i}
the value of
\mathscr{F}
on a morphism
[0]\rightarrow[m]
sending
0
to
i
and by
\mathscr{F}_{s,t}
the value on a morphism
[1]\rightarrow[m]
sending
0
to
s
and
1
to
t
. Further, denote

\mathop{\times}_{\mathscr{F}_{j}}^{n_{j}}\mathscr{F}_{j,j}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\overbrace{(\mathscr{F}_{j,j}\times_{\mathscr{F}_{j}}\mathscr{F}_{j,j}\times_{\mathscr{F}_{j}}...\times_{\mathscr{F}_{j}}\mathscr{F}_{j,j})}^{\text{$n_{j}$ times}}

and for
\overline{n^{i}}=\{n^{i}_{1},...,n^{i}_{f(i)-f(i-1)-1}\}\in\Delta^{f(i)-f(i-1)-1}

\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\overline{n^{i}})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathop{\times}_{\mathscr{F}_{f(i-1)+1}}^{n^{i}_{1}}\mathscr{F}_{f(i-1)+1,f(i-1)+1})\times_{\mathscr{F}_{f(i-1)+1,f(i-1)+2}}...\times_{\mathscr{F}_{f(i)-1,f(i)}}(\mathop{\times}_{\mathscr{F}_{f(i)-1}}^{n^{i}_{f(i)-f(i-1)-1}}\mathscr{F}_{f(i)-1,f(i)-1}).

With these notations, we need to prove that

\displaystyle i^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{m,!}\mathscr{F}\cong

\displaystyle\underset{\{\overline{n}^{i}\}_{i\in\{1,2,...,n\}}\in\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{f(i)-f(i-1)-1}}{\mathrm{colim}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\overline{n^{1}})\times_{\mathscr{F}_{f(1)}}...\times_{\mathscr{F}_{f(n-1)}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\overline{n^{n}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle(\underset{\overline{n}^{1}\in(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{f(1)-f(0)-1}}{\mathrm{colim}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\overline{n^{1}}))\times_{\mathscr{F}_{f(1)}}...\times_{\mathscr{F}_{f(n-1)}}(\underset{\overline{n}^{n}\in(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{f(n)-f(n-1)-1}}{\mathrm{colim}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\overline{n^{n}})).

Using that
\mathscr{S}
is an
\infty
-topos, we see that this colimit diagram can be viewed as a colimit diagram in
\mathscr{S}_{/\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathscr{F}_{i}}
and also that the limits appearing in
i^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{m,!}\mathscr{F}(f)
are given by finite products in
\mathscr{S}_{/\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathscr{F}_{i}}
. It will now suffice to prove that
(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{m-n}
-colimits distribute over finite products in
\mathscr{S}_{/\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathscr{F}_{i}}
, which follows from
[
CH19
, Lemma 7.15]
since
\mathscr{S}_{/\prod_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathscr{F}_{i}}
is
\times
-admissible.
∎

Notation 2.5
.

We will denote by
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
the category
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}}(\mathscr{S})
.

Construction 2.6
.

Define
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}
to be a category with objects given by cellular morphisms

s:\sqcup_{j=1}^{k}[n_{j}]\rightarrow[m]

and define morphisms between two objects to be morphisms of categories over
[m]
. We will call a morphism
inert
if its restriction to all disjoint components is inert in the usual sense and denote
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}^{\mathrm{int}}
the full subcategory on inert morphisms. We call an object
e:[l]\rightarrow[m]

elementary
if
l=0
or
l=1
and denote
j:(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}^{\mathrm{el}}\hookrightarrow(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}^{\mathrm{int}}
the inclusion of a full subcategory on elementary objects.

Lemma 2.7
.

\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
is isomorphic to the category of presheaves on
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}
whose restriction to
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}^{\mathrm{int}}
is the right Kan extension of its restriction to
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}^{\mathrm{el}}
.

Proof.

Observe that there is a natural inclusion of a full subcategory
i:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}\hookrightarrow(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}
. We will first prove that
i_{*}
takes Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
-spaces to presheaves satisfying the conditions of the lemma and that
i^{*}
is its inverse. By definition

i_{*}\mathscr{F}(s)\cong\underset{(s\rightarrow ix)\in s/i}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(x).

Denote by
(s/i)^{\mathrm{incl}}\hookrightarrow s/i
the full subcategory on inclusions of the form
[n_{i}]\hookrightarrow\sqcup_{j=1}^{k}[n_{j}]
, it is obviously coinitial in
s/i
and consists of
k
disjoint objects. It follows that

i_{*}\mathscr{F}(s)\cong\mathop{\times}\limits_{j=1}^{k}\mathscr{F}(s_{j}).

It is also easy to see that

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{el}}_{a,s/}\cong\mathop{\times}\limits_{j=1}^{k}(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{el}}_{a,s_{j}/},

so the claim follows. Now observe that the isomorphism
i^{*}i_{*}\cong\mathrm{id}
follows since
i
is a full subcategory inclusion and
i_{*}i^{*}\mathscr{F}\cong\mathscr{F}
for presheaves satisfying the conclusion of the lemma follows from the above considerations.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}\cong\underset{e\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[m]/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/e}.

Proof.

It follows from the previous lemma in view of the obvious isomorphism

(1)

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]})_{a}\cong\underset{e\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[m]/}}{\lim}(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/e})_{a}

which moreover respects elementary objects and inert morphisms.
∎

Proposition 2.9
.

There is a
\mathpzc{Cat}
-Segal space
\mathrm{Corr}
whose space of objects is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Cat}^{\sim}\cong(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[0]})^{\sim}
and whose space of morphisms is isomorphic to
(\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[1]})^{\sim}
.

Proof.

We first define
\mathrm{Corr}([m])\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
. The isomorphism of
2.8
then implies that this indeed defines a
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{int}}
-Segal space. We will now prove that for every
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
the natural morphism
f^{*}:\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}}(\mathscr{S})
restricts to
f^{*}:\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n]}}(\mathscr{S})
.

We first write the required condition more explicitly. Given
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}}(\mathscr{S})
and a morphism
g:[k]\rightarrow[n]
we have

f^{*}\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\mathscr{F}(f\circ g)\cong\underset{\widetilde{c}\in i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f\circ g}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(\widetilde{c}),

where the last isomorphism follows since
\mathscr{F}
is a
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
-Segal space. For it to be a
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n]}
-Segal space we need

f^{*}\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\underset{c\in i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(f\circ c)\cong\underset{c\in i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g}{\mathrm{colim}}\underset{c_{0}\in i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f\circ c}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(c_{0})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\underset{c_{0}\in i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f/g}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(c_{0}),

where
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f/g
is by definition a Cartesian fibration over
i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g
associated to a functor sending
c\rightarrow g
to
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f\circ c
. The objects of
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f/g
can be identified with pairs
(c\xrightarrow{\alpha}g,c_{0}\xrightarrow{\beta}f\circ c)
where both
c
and
c_{0}
are cellular. There is a natural functor from
m:i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f/g\rightarrow i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/f\circ g
given by sending
(c\xrightarrow{\alpha}g,c_{0}\xrightarrow{\beta}f\circ c)
to the composition
c_{0}\xrightarrow{\beta}f\circ c\xrightarrow{f*\alpha}f\circ g
and we will prove that
m
is cofinal.

To accomplish this, first recall the classical fact that every morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
in
\Delta
is expressible as a finite composition of face and degeneracy maps
\sigma_{i}
and
\delta_{j}
. It will be enough to prove our statement for any of those maps individually. We start with the face map
\delta_{j}:[n]\rightarrow[n+1]
. The object of
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/\delta_{j}/g
can be identified with a pair of commutative diagrams

\hbox to109.52pt{\vbox to70.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.76045pt\lower-35.3195pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.57158pt}{30.37366pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

For a given object
[l]\xrightarrow{\gamma}[k]
of
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/\delta_{j}\circ g
an object of
m/\gamma
can be identified with a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{j}}

in which
\eta\circ\beta\circ\alpha\cong\gamma
and a morphism is given by a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{1}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{2}^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\prime}}

over
[n+1]
. Observe that we can factor
[k_{1}]\xrightarrow{\gamma}[k]
canonically as the top row in the following diagram

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the square is a pullback square. Observe that
\mathrm{Im}(c)\cap\mathrm{Im}(\delta_{j})\neq\varnothing
since otherwise it would not admit a morphism from
\delta_{j}\circ g
. Since
\delta_{j}
is injective it follows from
lemma

4.27
that
\delta_{j}^{*}[k]
is indeed an object of
\Delta
, moreover it is easy to see that the pullback
\delta_{j}^{*}c
is also cellular. It follows that we can identify the diagram with an object of
m/\gamma
. Moreover, by construction this object is final, so in particular it follows that
m/\gamma
is contractible and
m:i_{n+1}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/\delta_{j}/g\rightarrow i_{n+1}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/\delta_{j}\circ g
is cofinal.

The case of a degeneracy map
\sigma_{i}:[n]\rightarrow[n-1]
is somewhat more difficult. First, observe that the composition of cellular maps is cellular and that
\sigma_{i}
is a cellular map. It follows that in this case the functor
m
restricts to
\widetilde{m}:i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g\rightarrow i_{n-1}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/\delta_{j}\circ g
, and it suffices to prove that this functor is cofinal. Observe that we can decompose every
\alpha:c\rightarrow g
as
c\overset{i_{\alpha}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{\alpha}\overset{a_{\alpha}}{\twoheadrightarrow}g
. It follows that the inclusions
i^{\mathrm{act}}:(i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g)^{\mathrm{act}}\hookrightarrow i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g
is cofinal. Moreover,
\widetilde{m}
restricts to
\widehat{m}:(i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g)^{\mathrm{act}}\rightarrow(i_{n-1}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/\delta_{j}\circ g)^{\mathrm{act}}
and it is enough to prove that
\widehat{m}
is cofinal. Next, observe that, since
\sigma_{i}^{*}
is a Segal morphism, we have

\sigma_{i}^{*}\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\underset{(g\rightarrowtail e)\in(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{el}}_{/g}}{\lim}\sigma_{i}^{*}\mathscr{F}(e),

so we can assume that
g
is a morphism from
e
to
[n]
where
e
is an elementary object of
\mathpzc{Cat}
. The case of
e=[0]
is trivial, so we can assume that
g
is a morphism
[1]\rightarrow[n]
. Denote the minimal subinterval of
[n]
containing the image of
g
by
[n_{g}]
, then since there is at most one active morphism to
[1]
from any object of
\mathpzc{Cat}
, it follows that
(i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}/g)^{\mathrm{act}}\cong\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/[n_{g}]}
, where
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{surj}}_{/[n_{g}]}
denotes the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n_{g}]}
on surjective cellular morphisms
[m]\rightarrow[n_{g}]
and active morphisms between them, and that a similar isomorphism holds for
\sigma_{i}\circ g
. Observe that the isomorphism of
2.8
restricts to the isomorphism

\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/[n_{g}]}\cong\underset{e\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[m]/}}{\lim}\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/e}.

Observe that the restriction of
\widehat{m}
to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/e}
is isomorphic to identity if
e
does not intersect with
\{i,i+1\}
inside
n_{g}
. It follows that it suffices to prove that
\widehat{m}
is cofinal when restricted to only those components that intersect
\{i,i+1\}
.

Now we have three cases to consider:
i=0
,
i=n-1
and
0<i<n-1
. The first and second case are entirely similar, so we only consider one of them. Assume
i=0
, then we are reduced to the following situation:
n=2
and
g
is the unique active morphism from
[1]
to
[2]
. Observe that in this case
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/[2]}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
and
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/[1]}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
. The functor
\widehat{m}
then sends
(a,n,b)
to
(a+n+1,b)
. Observe that we have the following diagram

Here both vertical morphisms are cofinal (since both

and
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
have a final object), so in order to prove that the top horizontal morphism is cofinal it suffices to prove that the bottom horizontal morphism is cofinal. This follows from
[

, Lemma 6.1.3.16.]
.

, then we are reduced to the following situation:

and

is the unique active morphism from

to
[3]
. Observe that in this case

and
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{Surj}}_{/[2]}\cong\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
. The functor
\widehat{m}
then sends
(a,n,m,b)
to
(a,n+m+1,b)
. Observe that we have the following diagram

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{119.56816pt}{-2.08334pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Here both vertical morphisms are again cofinal, so in order to prove that the top horizontal morphism is cofinal it suffices to prove that the bottom horizontal morphism is cofinal. Denote by
F:\Delta\times\Delta\rightarrow\Delta
the morphism sending
([k],[l])
to
[k+l+1]
, we need to prove it is coinitial, i.e. we need to prove that for any
[n]\in\Delta
the overcategory
\Delta_{F/[n]}
is contractible. An object of
\Delta_{F/[n]}
is given by the data of natural numbers
k
and
l
, a morphism
\alpha:[1]\rightarrow[n]
and a pair of morphisms
f:[k]\rightarrow[0,\alpha(0)]
and
g:[l]\rightarrow[\alpha(1),n]
. Denote by
i^{\mathrm{id}}:\Delta_{F/[n]}^{\mathrm{id}}\hookrightarrow\Delta_{F/[n]}
the inclusion of the full subcategory on those objects for which both
f
and
g
are identities. It admits an obvious left adjoint
L:\Delta_{F/[n]}\rightarrow\Delta_{F/[n]}^{\mathrm{id}}
sending
(k,l,\alpha,f,g)
to
(\alpha(0),(n-\alpha(1)),\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{[\alpha(0)]},\mathrm{id}_{[n-\alpha(1)]})
. In particular, there is a natural isomorphism

N(\Delta_{F/[n]})\xrightarrow{\sim}N(\Delta_{F/[n]}^{\mathrm{id}})

on geometric realizations, so it is enough to prove that
\Delta_{F/[n]}^{\mathrm{id}}
is contractible. The objects of
\Delta_{F/[n]}^{\mathrm{id}}
are easily seen to be uniquely determined by
\alpha:[1]\rightarrow[n]
, and there is a unique morphism from
\alpha
to
\beta
if and only if
\alpha(0)\leq\beta(0)\leq\beta(1)\leq\alpha(1)
. In particular, it admits an initial object given by the unique active morphism
a:[1]\twoheadrightarrow[n]
, so it follows that
\Delta_{F/[n]}^{\mathrm{id}}
is contractible.
∎

Notation 2.10
.

We will denote a morphism from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
in
\mathrm{Corr}
by
\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
and call it a
correspondence
from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
. We will also frequently denote the space
M(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})
by the same letter.

Remark 2.11
.

Observe that both the space of objects and the space of morphisms of
\mathrm{Corr}
are themselves categories, and moreover the unit and multiplication maps for
\mathrm{Corr}
are functors between those categories. This endows
\mathrm{Corr}
with a structure of a double Segal space. If we only consider those morphisms whose restriction to the category of objects are identity we get a structure of a twofold Segal space, and we will typically view
\mathrm{Corr}
as endowed with this structure.

Remark 2.12
.

Given a morphism
h:[k]\rightarrow[s]
, we can consider the induced functor from
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[s]}
to

Notation 2.13
.

Given
[m]\in\Delta
, denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[m]}
the algebraic pattern whose underlying category is
(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{op}}
, whose inert subcategory contains all morphism and whose active subcategory is trivial. Declare an object
[k]\rightarrowtail[m]
to be elementary if
k\in\{0,1\}
. Denote by
i_{m}^{\mathrm{int}}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[m]}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
the natural inclusion.

Proposition 2.14
.

i_{m}^{\mathrm{int}}
is an extendable morphism of algebraic patterns.

Proof.

Observe that
i_{m}^{\mathrm{int}}
has unique lifting of inert morphisms. The relative Segal condition follows from the relative Segal condition in
\mathpzc{Cat}
. Observe that for any
f\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[m]}
the category
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[m]})^{\mathrm{act}}_{/f}
is a singleton
\{f_{i}\}
containing the inert part of the active/inert decomposition of
f
. In particular, it is a Segal space.
∎

Proposition 2.15
.

For every
f:[n]\rightarrow[m]
the induced morphism
f^{*}:\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}}(\mathscr{S})
preserves the image of
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}\circ i_{m}^{\mathrm{int}}
.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathscr{F}\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[m]}
is in the essential image of
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}\circ i_{m}^{\mathrm{int}}
if and only if
\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\mathscr{F}(g_{i})
where
[l]\overset{g_{a}}{\twoheadrightarrow}[\overline{l}]\overset{g_{i}}{\rightarrowtail}[m]
is the active/inert factorization of
g
. By definition
f^{*}\mathscr{F}(h)\cong\mathscr{F}(f\circ h)
, so let
f\cong f_{i}\circ f_{a}
and
h\cong h_{i}\circ h_{a}
be the active/inert factorizations in
\Delta
and consider the following commutative diagram

\hbox to188.84pt{\vbox to123.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 94.41907pt\lower-61.9793pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Then we see that
f^{*}\mathscr{F}(h)\cong\mathscr{F}(f_{i}\circ\widetilde{h}\circ\widetilde{f}\circ h_{a})\cong\mathscr{F}(f_{i}\circ\widetilde{h})
since
\mathscr{F}
is in the image of of
i_{m}^{\mathrm{Cell}}\circ i_{m}^{\mathrm{int}}
. Similarly,
f^{*}\mathscr{F}(h_{i})\cong\mathscr{F}(f_{i}\circ\widetilde{h}\circ\widetilde{f})\cong\mathscr{F}(f_{i}\circ\widetilde{h})
, so in particular
f^{*}\mathscr{F}(h)\cong f^{*}\mathscr{F}(h_{i})
, so
f^{*}\mathscr{F}
also lies in the image of
i_{n}^{\mathrm{Cell}}\circ i_{n}^{\mathrm{int}}
.
∎

Corollary 2.16
.

There is a flagged bicategory
\mathrm{Span}
whose space of objects is given by
(\mathscr{S})^{\sim}
and whose category of morphisms is given by
\mathscr{P}((\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{int}}_{[1]})^{\mathrm{el}})\cong\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{int}}_{/[1]}}(\mathscr{S})
.

Proof.

Using
2.15
we construct
\mathrm{Span}
just as
\mathrm{Corr}
in
2.9
and endow it with the structure of a flagged bicategory as in
remark

2.11
.
∎

Proposition 2.17
.

\mathrm{Span}
can be identified with a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Corr}
on
\infty
-groupoids.

Proof.

Indeed, by definition every object of
\mathrm{Span}
is a groupoid. Conversely, if
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[1]}}\cong\mathrm{Corr}_{1}
is such that
\mathscr{F}(f)\cong\mathscr{F}(i)
, where
i\in\{0,1\}
and
f:[n]\rightarrow[1]
is a morphism sending every object of
[n]
to
i
, then we see that for arbitrary
g:[l]\rightarrow[m]
we either have
\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\mathscr{F}(i)
or
\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\mathscr{F}(g_{0})\times_{\mathscr{F}(0)}\mathscr{F}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})\times_{\mathscr{F}(1)}\mathscr{F}(g_{1})\cong\mathscr{F}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})
where
g_{i}
is a fiber of
g
over
i
(we merely applied the Segal condition). In particular, we have that
\mathscr{F}(g)\cong\mathscr{F}(g_{i})
, so
\mathscr{F}
belongs to
\mathrm{Span}_{1}
.
∎

Remark 2.18
.

It is possible to show that our flagged bicategory
\mathrm{Span}
is isomorphic to the faithful subcategory of
\mathrm{Span}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
in the notation of
[
Hau18a
]
having the same objects and morphisms, but only the 2-morphisms of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

3.
Basic properties of Corr

Our goal in this section is to prove certain basic properties of
\mathrm{Corr}
, most of which would be used in later chapters. We begin by defining correspondences
f^{*}
and
f_{!}
for every morphism
f
in
\mathrm{Cat}
in
3.6
and proving that this defines functors
\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}
in
3.7
and
3.28
. We also prove that they are adjoint to each other in
3.9
.

Our next important result is
3.18
that uses the construction of completion from
[
AF18
]
to provide for complete Segal spaces
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
an isomorphism between our category of correspondences
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
and
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}})
, thus proving that it extends the classical definition. Finally, in
3.23
we give a rather technical description of the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D},*)
for
\mathscr{D}\in\mathrm{Cat}_{/[n]}
that will be used in later sections.

Construction 3.1
.

Given a pair of Segal spaces
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
, consider the functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
([n],[m])
to
\mathscr{C}_{n}\times\mathscr{D}_{m}
. Denote by
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
the corresponding right fibration. Denote by
j:\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\infty}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
the inclusion of the subcategory containing only morphisms lying over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
and call those morphisms
inert
. Finally, declare objects lying over
([0],[0])

elementary
and denote by
i:\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{el}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\infty}
the inclusion of the full subcategory on inert morphisms. Define
\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
to be the category of presheaves
\mathscr{F}
on
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{op}}
such that
j^{*}\mathscr{F}\cong i_{*}i^{*}j^{*}\mathscr{F}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\cong\mathrm{Corr}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
and the forgetful functor
\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})
is monadic.

Proof.

We first observe that a correspondence
M:\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
can be equivalently described as a functor
F:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
such that
F([n],[m])\cong\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{m}
, where
M\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{0}\times\mathscr{C}_{0}
is the underlying space of the correspondence. Using the equivalence
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(X,\mathscr{S})\cong\mathscr{S}_{/X}
for
\infty
-groupoids and the fact that
([0],[0])
is final in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
, we see that those are exactly the presheaves satisfying the conditions of the lemma. To prove the monadicity claim we will prove that
j_{!}i_{*}
restricts to a functor
\mathscr{P}(\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{op}})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
, the monadicity would then follow as in
[
CH19
, Proposition 8.1]
.

To prove it observe that

j_{!}i_{*}M(c_{m},d_{n})\cong\underset{((c^{\prime}_{l},d^{\prime}_{k})\rightarrow(c_{m},d_{n}))\in\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\infty}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}}{\mathrm{colim}}i_{*}M(c^{\prime}_{l},d^{\prime}_{n}),

where
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\infty}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}
is the category with objects given by morphisms to
(c_{m},d_{n})
and morphisms by morphisms over
(c_{m},d_{n})
in
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\infty}
. First, observe that all active morphisms belong to
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
, so using the fact that
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
admits an active/inert factorization we see that it suffices to take the colimit over the full subcategory
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{int},\infty}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\infty}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}
on inert morphisms
((c^{\prime}_{l},d^{\prime}_{k})\rightarrowtail(c_{m},d_{n}))
.

Observe that any inert morphism that does nor lie over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
can be uniquely decomposed as
c_{\infty}\circ f
where
f
is a morphism lying over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
and
c_{\infty}
is a morphism lying over
s_{\infty}:([m],[n])\rightarrow([m+1],[n+1])
which (considered as a morphism in
\Delta\times\Delta
) sends
([m],[n])
isomorphically onto a subinterval
([1,m],[1,n])
. This means we can take the colimit over
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{s_{\infty},\mathrm{id}}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}
which is the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{int},\infty}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}
on morphisms lying over
s_{\infty}
or
\mathrm{id}
. Finally, observe that there is a morphism
\mathrm{id}\rightarrow s_{\infty}
in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{/([m],[n])}
, so the category
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{s_{\infty}}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}
of morphisms lying over
s_{\infty}
is cofinal in
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{s_{\infty},\mathrm{id}}_{/(c_{m},d_{n})}
. By untangling definitions, we finally see that
j_{!}i_{*}
sends
M\in\mathscr{S}_{/\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}}
to the presheaf sending
([n],[m])
to
\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{m}
, which is obviously and element of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
.
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

There is an isomorphism

\mathrm{Corr}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\cong\mathrm{Corr}(*,\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{D})

Proof.

First, observe that
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{el}}\cong\mathrm{Fib}(*,\mathscr{D}\times\mathscr{C})^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\mathscr{S}_{/\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}}
. It follows from the proof of
3.2
that the free correspondence monad on
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{\mathrm{el}}
is given by
M\mapsto\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}
and the claim follows since these monads are manifestly equivalent for
\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{Corr}}(*,\mathscr{D}\times\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}})
.
∎

Construction 3.4
.

Observe that given two algebraic patterns
\mathscr{O}
and
\mathscr{E}
, their product
\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{E}
also obtains a natural structure of an algebraic pattern such that the category of Segal
\mathscr{O}\times\mathscr{E}
-spaces is isomorphic to the category of Segal
\mathscr{O}
-objects in
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathscr{S})
. In particular, this applies to
\mathpzc{Cat}\times[n]
whose Segal spaces are given by strings of morphisms
\mathscr{C}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{n}
of
\mathpzc{Cat}
-Segal spaces. We define a functor
L_{1}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n]}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(\mathpzc{Cat}\times[n])
(resp.
L_{2}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n]}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}(\mathpzc{Cat}\times[n])
) sending
\alpha:[m]\rightarrow[n]
to
\alpha^{-1}(\{0\})\hookrightarrow\alpha^{-1}([0,1])\hookrightarrow...\hookrightarrow\alpha^{-1}([n])=[m]
(resp. to
\alpha^{-1}(\{n\})\hookrightarrow\alpha^{-1}([n-1,1])\hookrightarrow...\hookrightarrow\alpha^{-1}([n])=[m]
).

Proposition 3.5
.

Both
L_{i}
and their right adjoints
R_{i}
restrict to functors between the respective categories of Segal objects.

Proof.

We will only prove the claim for
L_{1}
and
R_{1}
, leaving a similar proof in the other case to the reader. First, observe that we can equivalently describe
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[n]}\xrightarrow{L_{1}}\mathpzc{Cat}\times[n]\xrightarrow{\{j\}^{*}}\mathpzc{Cat}
as given by the correspondence

where
i_{j}:[j]\hookrightarrow[n]
denotes the natural inclusion of
[j]
as an initial segment and
p:[j]\rightarrow[0]
is the unique morphism. Since all of the morphisms involved restrict to morphisms between the corresponding Segal categories, we see that the same is true of
L_{1}
as well. Conversely, given
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
and
\alpha:[m]\rightarrow[n]
we see that

R_{1}f(\alpha)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\Delta\times[n]}(L_{1}\alpha,f).

From the description of
L_{1}
we see that

L_{1}f(\alpha)\cong\underset{e\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell},\mathrm{el}}_{\alpha/}}{\mathrm{colim}}L_{1}f(e),

so
R_{1}
indeed satisfies the required property.
∎

Notation 3.6
.

Given
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
we will denote by
f_{!}:\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
the correspondence
R_{1}f
and by
f^{*}:\mathscr{D}\nrightarrow\mathscr{C}
the correspondence
R_{2}f
.

Proposition 3.7
.

The morphism sending
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
to
f^{*}
(resp. to
f_{!}
) extends to a functor
\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}
.

Proof.

We will prove the theorem for
(-)_{!}
, leaving a similar argument for
(-)^{*}
to the reader. We need to prove that for any morphism
\alpha:[n]\rightarrow[m]
in
\Delta
we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{54.77068pt}{-2.14153pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -30.34 -38.26 L 37.79 -38.26}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.51096pt}{-27.6498pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.29689pt}{-23.32481pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Observe that we can represent any
\alpha
as a composition of face and degeneracy maps, so it would suffice to prove the claim for those. First assume that
\alpha
is a degeneracy map
\sigma_{i}:[n]\rightarrow[n-1]
. Now observe that for any
\mathscr{F}\in[m]\times\mathrm{Cat}
and for any
c:[l]\rightarrow[n]
such that its image does not contain
i
we have
R_{1,n}\circ\sigma_{i}^{*}\mathscr{F}(c)\cong\sigma^{*}_{i,\mathrm{Cell}}\circ R_{1,n-1}\mathscr{F}(c)\cong R_{1,n-1}\mathscr{F}(\sigma_{i}\circ{c})
. So we only have to prove the statement for the morphisms
c
whose image contains
i
. Moreover, using that both
R_{1,n}\circ\sigma_{i}^{*}\mathscr{F}
and
\sigma^{*}_{i,\mathrm{Cell}}\circ R_{1,n-1}\mathscr{F}
satisfy the Segal condition, we can further assume that the image of
c
is contained in the interval
\{i-1,i\}
or
\{i,i+1\}
. Since in this case
c
uniquely factors through
[1]\hookrightarrow[n]
we are finally reduced to proving the statement for
\sigma_{)}:[1]\rightarrow[0]
. In other words, we have to prove that for any Segal space
\mathscr{C}
the morphism
R_{1}
sends
(\mathscr{C}=\joinrel=\mathscr{C})
to the identity correspondence, which is immediate.

Now we consider the case of the face map
\delta_{j}:[n-1]\rightarrow[n]
. Just like in the previous paragraph, we are reduced to proving the claim for the unique active morphism
[1]\rightarrow[2]
. In other words, for a composable pair
(\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{D}\xrightarrow{g}\mathscr{E})
we need to demonstrate that
g_{!}\circ f_{!}\cong(g\circ f)_{!}
. It would suffice to demonstrate
g_{!}\circ f_{!}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})\cong(g\circ f)_{!}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})
. We have seen in the course of the proof of
2.9
that
g_{!}\circ f_{!}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})
is isomorphic to the colimit of a functor
F:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
given by

F([n])\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1})\overbrace{\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}...\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}}^{\text{n times}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{E}_{0}}\mathscr{E}_{1}).

Note that this functor is a restriction along
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{[n]\rightarrow[n+1]}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
of a functor
\widetilde{F}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
[n]
to

\widetilde{F}([n])\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathscr{C}_{0})\overbrace{\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}...\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}}^{\text{n times}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{E}_{0}}\mathscr{E}_{1}).

Since
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
has a final object
[0]
, the colimit of this functor is isomorphic to

\widetilde{F}([0])\cong\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{E}_{0}}\mathscr{E}_{1}\cong(g\circ f)_{!}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]}).

On the other hand we know from the proof of
2.9
that
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}
is cofinal, so in the end we get

g_{!}\circ f_{!}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]})\cong\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}F([n])\cong\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}}{\mathrm{colim}}\widetilde{F}([n])\cong\widetilde{F}([0])\cong(g\circ f)_{!}(\mathrm{id}_{[1]}).

∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Given a correspondence
M:\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
, denote by
i_{1}:\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow M
and
i_{2}:\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow M
the natural inclusions of fibers over
0
and
1
. Then we have

M\cong i_{2}^{*}\circ i_{1,!}.

Proof.

First, observe that by definition

\begin{split}i_{2}^{*}\circ i_{1,!}\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{M_{0}}M_{1}\times_{M_{0}}M_{n}\times_{M_{0}}M_{1}\times_{M_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}M_{1}^{\mathscr{C}}\times_{M_{0}}M_{n}\times_{M_{0}}M_{1}^{\mathscr{D}}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}M^{n+2,\mathrm{surj}},\end{split}

where
M_{1}^{\mathscr{C}}
(resp.
M_{1}^{\mathscr{D}}
) denotes the space of morphisms of
M
with source in
\mathscr{C}
(resp. with target in
\mathscr{D}
) and
M^{n,\mathrm{surj}}
denotes the space of composable strings of
n
morphisms with source in
\mathscr{C}
and target in
\mathscr{D}
. Observe that
M^{n,\mathrm{surj}}
is the left Kan extension along
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{(n,m)\mapsto(n+m+1)}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
of the presheaf on
\Delta\times\Delta
sending
(n,m)
to
\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{m}
. By transitivity of Kan extensions, we can continue the above chain of isomorphisms as follows:

\begin{split}i_{2}^{*}\circ i_{1,!}\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}M^{n+2,\mathrm{surj}}\\
\cong&\underset{(n,m)\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}(\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{m})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{(n,m)\in\Delta_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta_{\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{m}\\
\cong&M,\end{split}

where the first isomorphism follows from the transitivity of Kan extensions, the second since
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
is cofinal and the last since
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}
has a final object.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

For any morphism
f:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
in
\mathrm{Cat}
the morphism
f_{!}
is left adjoint to
f^{*}
in
\mathrm{Corr}
.

Proof.

We begin by describing the unit and counit morphisms. We start with the counit
\epsilon:f_{!}f^{*}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}
:

\begin{split}f_{!}f^{*}\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}(\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{0})\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n})\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\xrightarrow{f}&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{n})\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{n+1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[m]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{m}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\cong&\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\cong&\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}},\end{split}

where the non-trivial isomorphisms follow by the same argument as in
3.8
.
Now we observe the following isomorphism:

\begin{split}f^{*}f_{!}\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0})\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{(n+1)}\\
\cong&\underset{[m]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{m}\\
\cong&\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0}.\end{split}

Using this isomorphism, we define
\eta:\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{D}}\rightarrow f^{*}f_{!}
as
\mathscr{D}_{1}\xrightarrow{(s,f,t)}\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0}
. Now it remains to verify the triangle identities. We first verify that
f_{!}\xrightarrow{\eta}f_{!}f^{*}f_{!}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}f_{!}
is isomorphic to the identity:

\begin{split}f_{!}\cong&\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})\\
\xrightarrow{f\times\mathrm{id}\times\mathrm{id}}&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})\\
\xrightarrow{f\times\mathrm{id}}&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{n+1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})\\
\cong&\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}.\end{split}

Since the restriction of all the morphisms to
(\mathscr{D}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})
is isomorphic to identity, we conclude that the whole composition is also isomorphic to identity. The other triangle identity is proved in a similar manner:

\begin{split}f^{*}\cong&\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\\
\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\mathrm{id}\times f}&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1})\\
\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times f}&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0})\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{n+1}\\
\cong&\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0},\end{split}

which is isomorphic to the identity for the same reason.
∎

Construction 3.10
.

Let
M:\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
be a correspondence. We will construct from it a simplicial space
\widetilde{M}
as follows: first consider the functor
F:\Delta\xrightarrow{\Delta}\Delta\times\Delta\rightarrow\Delta_{/[1]}
sending
[n]
to the morphism
f:[2n+1]\rightarrow[1]
that sends
[0,n]
to
0
, the segment
[n,n+1]
to
[0,1]
and the rest to
1
. We define
\widetilde{M}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}F^{*}M
, where
M
is viewed as a presheaf on
\Delta_{/[1]}
. Observe that there are natural transformations
\alpha:j_{i}\rightarrow F,i\in\{1,2\}
where
j_{i}:\Delta\rightarrow\Delta_{/[1]}
is a functor sending
[n]
to a constant morphism
[n]\rightarrow[1]
with the target
\{i\}
. It induces a natural functor
p:\widetilde{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Assume we are given an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
equipped with a monad
T
and a fixed object
x\in\mathscr{C}
. Then giving a structure of a
T
-algebra on
x
is equivalent to lifting
T
to a monad
\widetilde{T}
on
\mathscr{C}_{/x}
. The category of algebras for the monad
\widetilde{T}
is then equivalent to the category of
T
-algebras over
x
.

Proof.

First assume we have an endofunctor
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, then it is easy to see that lifting
F
to an endofunctor of
\mathscr{C}_{/x}
is equivalent to giving a morphism
m:Fx\rightarrow x
. Indeed, this is certainly necessary, and conversely, given a morphism like this we define
F(y\xrightarrow{f}x)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}Fy\xrightarrow{Ff}Fx\xrightarrow{m}x
. Similarly, assume we have a natural transformation
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
where both
F
and
G
lift to
\mathscr{C}_{/x}
. Then
\alpha
lifts to
\mathscr{C}_{/x}
if and only if the following diagram commutes

In other words, this is equivalent to giving
x
the structure of a
T
-algebra.

To prove the second claim, observe that the lifting of
T^{n}
to
\mathscr{C}_{/x}
sends
f:y\rightarrow x
to
T^{n}y\xrightarrow{T^{n}f}T^{n}x\xrightarrow{m^{n}}x
, so giving
f
the structure of a
T
-algebra is equivalent to giving a morphism
m_{y}:Ty\rightarrow y
endowing
y
with the
T
-algebra structure such that it makes the following diagram commute

\hbox to121.55pt{\vbox to68.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.77553pt\lower-32.83336pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

This is manifestly the same data necessary to give

a structure of a

-algebra over
