.
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

\widetilde{M}
is a Segal space. Moreover, postcomposition with
p_{!}p^{*}
induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(*,\widetilde{M})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})_{/M}
.

Proof.

First, observe that
F
is
not
a morphism of algebraic patterns. We will prove that

\widetilde{M}([n])\cong\underset{e\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[n]/}}{\lim}\widetilde{M}(e)

by induction on
n
. Observe that
\widetilde{M}([0])\cong M
and
\widetilde{M}([1])\cong\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\cong M\times_{(\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0})}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times\mathscr{D}_{1})
. Assume that the claim is true for
(n-1)
, then we have

\begin{split}\widetilde{M}([n])\cong&M\times_{(\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0})}(\mathscr{C}_{n}\times\mathscr{D}_{n})\\
\cong&M\times_{(\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0})}(\mathscr{C}_{n-1}\times\mathscr{D}_{n-1})\times_{M}(M\times_{(\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0})}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times\mathscr{D}_{1}))\\
\cong&\widetilde{M}([n-1])\times_{\widetilde{M}[0]}\widetilde{M}([1]),\end{split}

so
\widetilde{M}
is indeed a Segal object.

To prove the last statement we will use
3.2
. Observe that
\mathrm{Fib}(*,\widetilde{M})^{\mathrm{el}}\cong\mathscr{S}_{/M}\cong(\mathscr{S}_{/\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}})_{/M}
. Moreover, the free
\widetilde{M}
-fibration monad is given by

K\mapsto K\times_{M}M\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times\mathscr{D}_{1})\cong K\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}\times\mathscr{D}_{0}}(\mathscr{C}_{1}\times\mathscr{D}_{1}).

So in particular it is a lifting of the free
(\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{D}^{\mathrm{op}})
-fibration monad to
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})_{/M}^{\mathrm{el}}
, so the last claim of the proposition now follows from
lemma

3.11
.
∎

Proposition 3.13
.

If we denote by
p_{1}:\widetilde{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
and
p_{2}:\widetilde{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
the natural projections, there is a natural isomorphism

M\cong p_{2,!}\circ p_{1}^{*}

Proof.

Observe that by definition we have

\begin{split}p_{2,!}\circ p_{1}^{*}\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{M}M_{n}\times_{m}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\\
\cong&M,\end{split}

where the isomorphisms follow as in
3.8
.
∎

Definition 3.14
.

Denote by
c_{1}
a Segal space with two objects and an invertible morphism between them. We call a Segal space
\mathscr{C}

complete
if any morphism
c_{1}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
factors through
e:\mathscr{C}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}
. We denote the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Cat}
on complete Segal spaces by
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{comp}}
and the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Corr}
on complete Segal spaces by
\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{comp}}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

The natural inclusion
i^{\mathrm{comp}}:\mathrm{Cat^{comp}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
admits a left adjoint
(\widehat{-}):\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat^{comp}}
. Moreover, for any Segal space
\mathscr{C}
we have
\mathscr{C}_{1}\cong\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}_{0}}}\widehat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}_{0}}}\mathscr{C}_{0}
.

Proof.

This is a combination of
[
AF18
, Lemma 1.9]
and
[
AF18
, Lemma 1.14]
.
∎

Proposition 3.16
.

The natural morphism
p:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{C}}
induces an isomorphism
p_{!}
in
\mathrm{Corr}
.

Proof.

We will prove that
p^{*}
constitutes an inverse to
p_{!}
. We know from the proof of
3.9
that

p^{*}p_{!}\cong\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}_{0}}}\widehat{\mathscr{C}_{1}}\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}_{0}}}\mathscr{C}_{0}\cong\mathscr{C}_{1}

where the last isomorphism follows from
3.15
. Similarly, we have

\begin{split}p_{!}p^{*}\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{1}\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{n})\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[n]\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{n+1})\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{1}\\
\cong&\underset{[m]\in\Delta_{-\infty}^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{m})\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{1}\\
\cong&\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}\times_{\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{1}\cong\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{1}\end{split}

where we once again used the isomorphism of
3.15
.
∎

Corollary 3.17
.

The functor
(\widehat{-}):\mathrm{Cat}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{comp}}
extends to a functor
(\widehat{-}):\mathrm{Corr}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathrm{comp}}
.

Proof.

Given a correspondence
M\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
we define

\widehat{M}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}p_{\mathscr{D},!}\circ M\circ p^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}:\widehat{\mathscr{C}}\nrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{D}}.

Since by
3.16
we have
p^{*}p_{!}\cong\mathrm{id}
and
p_{!}p^{*}\cong\mathrm{id}
, we see that this indeed defines a functor.
∎

Corollary 3.18
.

Given two Segal spaces
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\cong\mathscr{P}(\widehat{\mathscr{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\widehat{\mathscr{C}})

Proof.

We know from
3.16
that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\widehat{\mathscr{C}},\widehat{\mathscr{D}})
, so it suffices to prove it for complete Segal spaces, in which case it follows from
[
AF17
, Lemma 4.1]
.
∎

Proposition 3.19
.

Assume that
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
is a morphism of Segal spaces such that the induced morphism
F_{0}:\mathscr{C}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{0}
is an effective epimorphism. Then
F_{!}:\mathrm{Corr}(*,\mathscr{C})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Corr}(*,\mathscr{D}):F^{*}
is a monadic adjunction.

Proof.

It follows from
3.16
that it suffices to prove the statement fro
\widehat{F}:\widehat{\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{D}}
. In this case it would follow from
[
CH19
, Proposition 8.1.]
once we prove that
\widehat{F}
induces an effective epimorphism on the space of objects. Observe that we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{C}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{D}}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{F}}

In this diagram all morphisms except possibly the right vertical map are effective epimorphisms, so it now follows from the two-out-of-three property
[
Lur09
, Corollary 6.2.3.12.]
that
\widehat{F}
is also an effective epimorphism.
∎

Construction 3.20
.

For a given
n\geq 0
denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
the full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}
on injective morphisms
g:[m]\rightarrow[n]
and by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
only containing active morphisms. Assume that we have a Segal space
(F:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow[n])\in\mathrm{Cat}_{/[n]}
, denote
\mathscr{D}_{i}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}F^{-1}(i)
. Also for
i\leq j\leq k\leq n
denote by
\mathscr{D}_{i,j}
the pullback
[1]\times_{[n]}\mathscr{D}
, where
[1]\rightarrow[n]
is the morphism sending
0
to
j
and
1
to
k
. We can view this as a correspondence from
\mathscr{D}_{j}
to
\mathscr{D}_{k}
. Denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
containing the same objects and only those morphisms that preserve the maximal element, observe that
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
is a subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
.

Now fix an object
\mathscr{F}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(\mathscr{F}_{0},\mathscr{F}_{1},...,\mathscr{F}_{n})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\coprod_{i=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{i},*)
. Denote by
\mathrm{Corr}_{n}^{\mathscr{F}}
the category whose objects are given by injective morphisms
g:[m]\rightarrow[n]
and a morphism from
f:[l]\rightarrow[n]
to
g
is given by a morphism
h
as in the diagram below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

together with the data of morphisms

\alpha_{h,i}:\mathscr{D}_{f(h(i+1)-1),g(i+1)}\circ\mathscr{D}_{f(h(i+1)-2),f(h(i+1)-1)}\circ...\circ\mathscr{D}_{g(i),f(h(i)+1)}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{g(i),g(i+1)}

for
0\leq i\leq m
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D}_{g(i)},\mathscr{D}_{g(i+1)})
and a morphism

\alpha_{h}:\mathscr{F}_{f(l)}\circ\mathscr{D}_{f(l)-1,f(l)}\circ...\circ\mathscr{D}_{g(m),g(m)+1}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}_{g(m)}

in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D}_{g(m)},*)
with the obvious operation of composition.

There is a natural forgetful functor
p_{\mathscr{F}}:\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathscr{F}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
. Observe that its pullback
p_{\mathscr{F}}^{-1}(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]})\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
along the natural inclusion
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
admits a section

s_{\mathscr{F},0}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}\rightarrow p^{-1}(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]})

that is identity on objects and sends every morphism
h
as above to the morphism in
\mathrm{Corr}_{n}^{\mathscr{F}}
for which
\alpha_{h}:\mathscr{F}_{g(m)}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}_{g(m)}
is the identity and every
\alpha_{h,i}
is induced by the composition maps. Now we define the category
M_{n}
to have as objects pairs
(\mathscr{F},s)
where
s
is a section of
p_{\mathscr{F}}
that restricts to
s_{\mathscr{F},0}
on the subcategory
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
. To define morphisms in
M_{n}
, observe that for any morphism
F:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\coprod_{i=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{i},*)
we can define the category
\mathrm{Corr}^{F}_{n}
exactly as above except we require the morphisms
\alpha_{h}
to be given by the commutative diagrams of the form

\hbox to261.81pt{\vbox to74.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 128.55887pt\lower-35.70836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Observe that there are forgetful functors
p_{1}:\mathrm{Corr}^{F}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathscr{F}}_{n}
and
p_{2}:\mathrm{Corr}^{F}_{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathscr{E}}_{n}
. We define the space of morphisms from
(\mathscr{F},s_{\mathscr{F}})
to
(\mathscr{E},s_{\mathscr{E}})
is given by the space of sections of
p_{F}:\mathrm{Corr}^{F}_{n}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
that extend
s_{F,0}
and such that
p_{1}\circ s_{F}\cong s_{\mathscr{F}}
and
p_{2}\circ s_{F}\cong s_{\mathscr{E}}
.

Construction 3.21
.

Assume that we are given a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
with a monad
T
on it and an object
x\in\mathscr{C}
. Then we can define a Segal space
\mathscr{C}^{T}_{x}
whose objects are given by numbers
n\geq 0
and such that a morphism from
l
to
m
is given by the data of a morphism
h:[m]\rightarrow[l]
together with natural transformations
\alpha_{h,i}:T^{h(i+1)-h(i)}\rightarrow T
and a morphism
\alpha_{h}:T^{l-h(m)}x\rightarrow x
. Denote by
p_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{T}_{x}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
, observe that there is a natural section

s_{x,0}:(\Delta^{\infty})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow p_{x}^{-1}((\Delta^{\infty})^{\mathrm{op}})

sending every morphism
h
to the morphism in
\mathscr{C}^{T}_{x}
for which
\alpha_{x}\cong\mathrm{id}
and all
\alpha_{h,i}
are given by the structural morphisms of
T
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Giving an object
x\in\mathscr{C}
, giving it a structure of a
T
-algebra is equivalent to extending the section
s_{x,0}
to a section
s_{x}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{T}_{x}
in the notation of
3.21
. A monad
T
on
\mathscr{C}
induces a monad
T_{\mathrm{Ar}}
on
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})
sending every
f:x\rightarrow y
to
Tf:Tx\rightarrow Ty
with the structural morphisms given by the naturality squares for the respective morphisms of
T
. If both
x
and
y
are
T
-algebras, then giving
f
a structure of a morphism of
T
-algebras is equivalent to giving a section
s_{f}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})_{f}^{T_{\mathrm{Ar}}}
that restricts to
s_{x}
and
s_{y}
on
\mathscr{C}^{T}_{x}
and
\mathscr{C}^{T}_{y}
respectively.

Proof.

By definition, a monad is an algebra in the monoidal category
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})
. For every Segal space
\mathscr{D}
the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{C})
is a right module over
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})
, where the action is given by postcomposition with an endomorphism. In particular, this applies to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(*,\mathscr{C})\cong\mathscr{C}
. Using
[
Lur
, Proposition 4.2.2.9.]
and
[
Lur
, 4.2.2.12.]
and untangling the definitions, we see that this structure can be encoded as a coCartesian fibration
M\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times[1]
such that
M([n],0)\cong\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})^{\times n}\times\mathscr{C}
,
M([n],1)\cong\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})^{\times n}
, the morphisms
([n],0)\rightarrow([n],1)
are sent to the natural projection
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})^{\times n}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})^{\times n}
, the restrictions to
\Delta\times\{1\}
and
\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}}\times\{0\}
are given by the structure of the monoidal category on
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})
, inert morphisms
i:[m]\rightarrowtail[n]
in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\{0\}
that preserve the maximal element are given by projections
\mathrm{End}^{\times m}\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}^{\times n}\times\mathscr{C}
. Giving a monad
T
with an algebra
x
is then equivalent to giving the section
s
of the natural projection
p:M\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times[1]
. Now observe that this section is in fact uniquely determined by its restriction to
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\{0\}
. Moreover, if we are given a monad
T
and an underlying object
x
of the prospective
T
-algebra, we can in fact restrict to considering sections of the natural projection from the full subcategory of
M
on
T
and
x
. It now follows by easy inspection that this space of sections is in fact the same as the one described in the statement of the lemma.

To prove the second claim we first observe that by definition the morphisms between
T
-algebras
x
and
y
are given by the natural transformations between sections
s_{x}
and
s_{y}
that restrict to identity on
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})^{\times n}
. For a pair of functors
F_{0},F_{1}:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathscr{E}
over a give category
\mathscr{C}
, the space of natural transformations between them is the space of morphisms
\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{E})
that restrict to
F_{1}
and
F_{1}
, where
\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{E})
is given by the following pullback square

\hbox to143.43pt{\vbox to68.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 71.71535pt\lower-34.15282pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

It is easy to see that if
\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
is a coCartesian fibration, then so is
\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{E})
and its fiber over
c\in\mathscr{C}
is given by
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{E}_{c})
. Applying it to
p:M\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times[1]
we see that
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})
also obtains the natural action of
\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})
. It is easy to see that this natural action is in fact given by

\mathrm{End}(\mathscr{C})\times\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})\xrightarrow{\Delta}\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C}))\times\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})

where
\Delta
is a morphism sending an endomorphism
G
of
\mathscr{C}
to an endomorphism of
\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})
that sends
c\xrightarrow[f]{c}^{\prime}
to
Gc\xrightarrow{Gf}Gc^{\prime}
. Putting everything together, we see that extending a given morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
to a morphism of
T
-algebras is equivalent to giving a section
s_{f}:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}(\mathscr{C})^{T_{\mathrm{Ar}}}_{f}
satisfying the properties described in the claim.
∎

Proposition 3.23
.

Given a Segal space
(F:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow[n])
as in
3.20
, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D},*)\cong M_{n}

in the notation of
3.20
.

Proof.

Consider the natural inclusion
j:\coprod_{k=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}
. It is an isomorphism on the space of objects, so by
3.19
the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D},*)
is monadic over
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\coprod_{j=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{j},*)
. It is easy to see that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\coprod_{k=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{k},*)\cong\prod_{k=0}^{n}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D}_{k},*)
. Denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[n]}
(resp.
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty}_{/[n]}
) the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}
containing only active (resp. maximal element preserving) morphisms over
[n]
and by
\widetilde{M}_{n}
the category defined exactly like
M_{n}
but with
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
and
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty,\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
replaced with
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[n]}
and
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\infty}_{/[n]}
. Using the calculations of
3.9
we see that

j^{*}j_{!}\cong\coprod_{k=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{k,0}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{1}}\coprod_{k=0}^{n}\mathscr{D}_{k,0}\cong\coprod_{f:[1]\rightarrow[n]}\mathscr{D}_{f(0),f(1)}.

Iterating this calculation we see that the
i
th component of
(j^{*}j_{!})^{m}\mathscr{F}
is given by

\coprod_{f:[m+1]\rightarrow[n],f(0)=i}\mathscr{F}_{f(m+1)}\circ\mathscr{D}_{f(m),f(m+1)}\circ\mathscr{D}_{f(m-1),f(m)}\circ...\circ\mathscr{D}_{i,f(1)}

and the unit and multiplication morphisms are given by composition and identity morphisms in
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[n]}
.

Observe that the left fibration over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
corresponding to a functor sending
[m]
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([m],[n])
is given by the natural projection
p:\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}
. It follows from this and
lemma

3.22
that the category of
j^{*}j_{!}
-algebras is isomorphic to
\widetilde{M}_{n}
. Now it remains to prove that the sections
s_{\mathscr{F}}:\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}\rightarrow\mathrm{Corr}^{\mathscr{F}}_{n}
in
\widetilde{M}_{n}
are uniquely defined by their restriction to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{inj}}_{/[n]}
, however this is obvious from the existence of surjective/injective factorization and the fact that all surjective morphisms in
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[n]}
are sent to identities.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

Applying
3.23
to the special case
n=1
we see that given a correspondence
M:\mathscr{C}\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
, the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(M,*)
(where here we denote by
M
the total category of the correspondence) is isomorphic to the category of triples
(\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{C}},\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{D}},\alpha:\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{D}}\circ M\rightarrow\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{C}})
where
\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{C}}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},*)
and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{D}}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{D},*)
.

Corollary 3.25
.

There is a natural equivalence between that space of natural transformations
\alpha:M\rightarrow N
where
M
and
N
are correspondences from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
and the
space of correspondences
\overline{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Corr}([1]\times\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
restricting to
M
and
N
on
0
and
1
respectively.

Proof.

Observe that
[1]\times\mathscr{C}
is equivalently the correspondence given by
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C},!}
. The result now follows from
remark

3.24
and
3.3
. For future use we will also explicate the construction. Assume that we are given a correspondence
\mathscr{C}\times[1]\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
restricting to
M
and
N
on the ends of the interval. Observe that the objects of
\mathrm{Fib}([1]\times\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
lying over
([m],[n])\in\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
are given by sequences

\widehat{c}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{m^{\prime}}\rightarrow c\rightarrow c^{\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c^{\prime}_{m^{\prime\prime}},d_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow d_{n})

for all
m^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime\prime}
such that
m^{\prime}+m^{\prime\prime}+1=m
. In particular, the morphisms
i_{1,2}:\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C}\times[1],\mathscr{D})
corresponding to the natural inclusions
i_{1,2}:\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\times[1]
are given by sending an object

(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{m},d_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow d_{n})\in\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})

to the same string of morphisms viewed as an element of
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C}\times[1],\mathscr{D}_{n})
for which
m^{\prime}=0
(resp.
m^{\prime\prime}=0
). To get the value of the corresponding natural transformation
\alpha
on
(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{m},d_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow d_{n})\in\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
, consider the objects

\overline{c}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{m},d_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow d_{n})

with
m^{\prime}=m-1
and
m^{\prime\prime}=0
and

\widetilde{c}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{m}=\joinrel=c_{m},d_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow d_{n})

with
m^{\prime}=m-1
and
m^{\prime\prime}=1
. Observe that there is a natural morphism
\widetilde{c}\rightarrow\overline{c}
ling over
([m],[n])\xrightarrow{(i_{m},\mathrm{id})}([m+1],[n])
where
i_{m}:[m]\rightarrowtail[m+1]
sends
[m]
isomorphically into the initial subinterval of
[m+1]
. This induces the morphism

\alpha:\mathscr{C}_{m}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{m}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}N\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}

which we declare the value of the required natural transformation.

Conversely, assume that we are given a natural transformation
\alpha:M\rightarrow N
. We define the corresponding functor
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C}\times[1],\mathscr{D})\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
by first sending every object of the form of
\widehat{c}
above with
m^{\prime\prime}>0
to
M
and objects as with
m^{\prime\prime}=0
to
N
. Then we need to define the action of morphisms. First, observe that we only need to define the action of morphisms whose image in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
does not belong to
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{\infty}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
. So assume we are given an object
\widehat{c}
as above and an inert morphism
([m_{1}],[n])\overset{(i,\mathrm{id})}{\rightarrowtail}([m],[n])
such that
i(m_{1})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}m_{2}<m^{\prime}
, then we define the corresponding morphism as a composition

\mathscr{C}_{m}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\overset{(j_{m^{\prime}},\mathrm{id})}{\rightarrowtail}\mathscr{C}_{m^{\prime}+1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathscr{C}_{m^{\prime}+1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}N\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\overset{(j_{m_{1}},\mathrm{id})}{\rightarrowtail}\mathscr{C}_{m_{1}}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}N\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}

where
j_{m^{\prime}}:[m^{\prime}+1]\rightarrowtail[m]
is the inclusion of
[m^{\prime}+1]
as the initial segment of
[m]
with the corresponding morphism

\mathscr{C}_{m}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\overset{(j_{m^{\prime}},\mathrm{id})}{\rightarrowtail}\mathscr{C}_{m^{\prime}+1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}M\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}

given by the structure of the correspondence on
M
and
j_{m_{1}};[m_{1}]\rightarrowtail[m^{\prime}+1]
is such that
j_{m_{1}}\circ j_{m^{\prime}}\cong i
with the corresponding morphism

\mathscr{C}_{m^{\prime}+1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}N\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}\overset{(j_{m_{1}},\mathrm{id})}{\rightarrowtail}\mathscr{C}_{m_{1}}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}N\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{n}

given by the structure of the correspondence on
N
.
∎

Lemma 3.26
.

Given an
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
together with two morphisms
\mathscr{F}:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{E}:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
, denote by
p_{1}:\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
(resp.
p_{2}:\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
) the left fibration over
\mathscr{C}
corresponding to
\mathscr{E}
(resp. to
\mathscr{F}
) and consider a functor
\overline{\mathscr{F}}:\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
e\in\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}
to
\mathscr{F}(p(e))
. Then the space of natural transformation
\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{F})
is isomorphic to the limit of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that the left fibration corresponding to
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
is
\overline{p}:\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}\times\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}
. By
[
Lur09
, Corollary 3.3.3.4.]
the limit of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
is given by the space of coCartesian sections of
\overline{p}
, which is easily seen to be the space of morphisms
\widetilde{\mathscr{E}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}
taking coCartesian edges to coCartesian edges, which is the same as
\mathrm{Nat}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{F})
.
∎

Lemma 3.27
.

Given two functors
F,G:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
between Segal spaces, consider the correspondence
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
viewed as a left fibration over
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
. Define a functor
\widetilde{G}:\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
that sends

(\overline{c_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{c_{2}}\rightarrow...\rightarrow\overline{c_{k}}\rightarrow c\rightarrow c^{\prime}\rightarrow\widehat{c_{1}}\rightarrow...\rightarrow\widehat{c_{t}})

to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(c),G(c^{\prime}))
. Then the space of natural transformations
\mathrm{Nat}(F,G)
is isomorphic to the limit of this functor.

Proof.

A natural transformation between
F
and
G
is a functor
[1]\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
that restricts to
F
on
\mathscr{C}\times\{0\}
and to
G
on
\mathscr{C}\times\{1\}
. Using
lemma

3.26
, we can view the space of morphisms
[1]\times\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
as the limit over
(\mathscr{C}\times[1])
(viewed as a left fibration over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
) of a functor
F_{\mathscr{D}}:(\mathscr{C}\times[1])\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending

\widetilde{c}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}(c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}...\xrightarrow{f_{n-1}}c_{n})\in(\mathscr{C}\times[1])_{n}

to
\mathscr{D}_{n}
. The space of natural transformations is then given by the limit of
\widetilde{F_{\mathscr{D}}}\subset F_{\mathscr{D}}
which sends
\widetilde{c}
as above to the space of sequences
(d_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}d_{2}\xrightarrow{g_{2}}...\xrightarrow{g_{n-1}}d_{n})
for which
d_{i}\cong F(c_{i})
(resp.
G(c_{i})
) and
g_{j}\cong F(f_{j})
(resp.
G(f_{j})
) if
c_{i}
and
f_{j}
are in the image of
\mathscr{C}\times\{0\}
(resp.
\mathscr{C}\times\{1\}
). Observe that since
\mathscr{C}\times[1]
is the total category of the identity correspondence, we have
(\mathscr{C}\times[1])\cong p_{!}\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
where
p:(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
sends
([n],[m])
to
[n+m+1]
and
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}(n,m)\cong\mathscr{C}_{n}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{m}
. In other words, there is an isomorphism

\coprod_{k+t=n-1}\mathscr{C}_{k}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{t}\cong(\mathscr{C}\times[1])_{n}.

Finally, observe that the functor

\coprod_{k+t=n-1}\mathscr{C}_{k}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{C}_{t}\cong(\mathscr{C}\times[1])_{n}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{F_{\mathscr{D}}}}\mathscr{S}

sends
(\overline{c_{1}}\rightarrow\overline{c_{2}}\rightarrow...\rightarrow\overline{c_{k}}\rightarrow c\rightarrow c^{\prime}\rightarrow\widehat{c_{1}}\rightarrow...\rightarrow\widehat{c_{t}})
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(F(c),G(c^{\prime}))
, so the resulting left fibration over
\mathrm{Fib}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
is indeed the one described in the claim.
∎

Proposition 3.28
.

Given two morphisms
f
and
g
from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Nat}(f,g)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})}(f_{!},g_{!})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})}(g^{*},f^{*})

Proof.

We will prove the proposition for
(-)_{!}
, the case of
(-)^{*}
would then follow from
3.9
and the elementary properties of adjunctions. First, observe that by definition
f_{!}\cong R_{1}f
for the functor
R_{1}
of
3.4
. It follows that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[1]}}(f_{!},g_{!})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[1]}}(R_{1}f,R_{1}g)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathpzc{Cat}\times[1]}(L_{1}R_{1}f,g).

Observe that
L_{1}R_{1}f
is given by the natural inclusion
i:\mathscr{C}\rightarrowtail f_{!}
of
\mathscr{C}
into the total category of the correspondence
f_{!}
. More explicitly, the value of
L_{1}R_{1}f
on
([n],0)
is given by
\mathscr{C}_{n}
and on
([m],1)
it is given by

p_{!}(f_{!})([n])\cong\coprod_{k+t=n-1}\mathscr{C}_{k}\times_{\mathscr{C}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{1}\times_{\mathscr{D}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{t}.

Denote by
F_{g}:L_{1}R_{1}f\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
the functor whose limit gives the space
\mathrm{Nat}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(f_{!},g_{!})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})}(f_{!},g_{!})
. By arguing as in the proof of
lemma

3.27
, we see that

F_{g}(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{n},0)\cong\{f(c_{n})\}

and

F_{g}(d_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow d_{t},d_{t}\rightarrow f(c_{1}),c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{k})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(d_{t},g(c_{1})).

Observe that there is a natural morphism
F:\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow L_{1}R_{1}f
sending

(c_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c_{t},c_{t}\rightarrow c^{\prime}_{1},c^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c^{\prime}_{k})

to

(f(c_{1})\rightarrow...\rightarrow f(c_{t}),f(c_{t})\rightarrow f(c^{\prime}_{1}),c^{\prime}_{1}\rightarrow...\rightarrow c^{\prime}_{k},1).

It follows by inspection that
\widetilde{g}\cong F_{g}\circ F
, where
\widetilde{g}:\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is the functor of
lemma

3.27
, and so we have a natural morphism

G:\mathrm{Nat}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(f_{!},g_{!})\cong\underset{L_{1}R_{1}f}{\lim}F_{g}\rightarrow\underset{\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\;\widetilde{g}\cong\mathrm{Nat}(f,g).

Conversely, given a natural transformation
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
we can view it as a morphism
\alpha:\mathscr{C}\times[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
and define
H:\mathrm{Nat}(f,g)\rightarrow\mathrm{Nat}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(f_{!},g_{!})
by sending
\alpha
to
\alpha_{!}:\mathscr{C}\times[1]\nrightarrow\mathscr{D}
which corresponds to an element of
\mathrm{Nat}_{\mathrm{Corr}}(f_{!},g_{!})
by
3.25
.

It is easy to see by untangling the definitions that
G\circ H\cong\mathrm{id}
. Given an element
\beta:f_{!}\rightarrow g_{!}
, which we view as a cartesian section of
F_{g}
, we see that
H\circ G(\beta)
sends
(d\xrightarrow{h}f(c))
to

d\xrightarrow{h}f(c)\xrightarrow{\beta(\mathrm{id}_{f(c)})}g(c)\in F_{g}(h)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{D}}(d,g(c)).

Observe that we have a sequence of morphisms

F_{g}(f(c)=\joinrel=f(c))\cong F_{g}(d\xrightarrow{h}f(c),f(c)=\joinrel=f(c))\rightarrow F_{g}(d\xrightarrow{h}f(c))

which in particular shows that for every
\beta\in\underset{L_{1}R_{1}f}{\lim}F_{g}
we have
\beta(h)\cong\beta(\mathrm{id}_{f(c)})\circ h
proving that indeed
H\circ G\cong\mathrm{id}
.
∎

Remark 3.29
.

3.28
combined with
3.7
shows that we can view
\mathrm{Cat}
as a faithful subcategory of
\mathrm{Corr}
.

4.
Lax functors

Our goal in this section is to describe the concept of a lax functor between a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
and a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
and prove their basic properties. In defining the lax functors we follow the approach of
[
GR17
]
, however for convenience we first describe in
4.10
for a category
\mathscr{C}
with a subcategory
\mathscr{D}
such that
\mathscr{C}_{0}\cong\mathscr{D}_{0}
the pattern
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
whose Segal spaces correspond to categories over
\mathscr{C}
that admit coCartesian lifts of all morphisms in
\mathscr{D}
. We then use this category to prove in
4.23
and
4.26
that the categories of lax and unital lax functors from a given
\mathscr{C}
are corepresentable by a twofold Segal spaces
L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
and
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}\mathscr{C}
respectively.

Finally, in
4.37
we introduce the concepts of lax cocones and lax colimits that will play a major role in the next section. We believe that our notion of lax colimits generalizes that of
[
GHN15
]
, however we do not prove that this is the case.

Proposition 4.1
.

There is a natural equivalence between the category of two-fold Segal spaces and the category of Cartesian fibrations over
\Delta
satisfying the Segal condition such that
F([0])
is a groupoid.

Proof.

First, observe that the category of Cartesian fibrations over
\Delta
satisfying the Segal condition can be equivalently described as the category of Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}\times\mathpzc{Cat}
-spaces in the notation of
3.4
. This is precisely the category of double Segal spaces. The fact that double Segal categories satisfying the conditions of the lemma describe twofold Segal spaces follows from
[
Hau18b
]
.
∎

Construction 4.2
.

We will denote by
2-\mathrm{Cat}
the Segal space of bicategories and functors between them. We will upgrade it to a flagged tricategory. In order to do this it is enough to endow each
\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})
with the structure of a flagged bicategory in a manner compatible with composition. To do this, first denote for
m\geq 0
by
\theta_{0,m}
the singleton category
[0]
and by
\theta_{1,m}
the bicategory with two objects
0
and
1
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{1,m}}(0,0)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{1,m}}(1,1)=\{\mathrm{id}\}
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{1,m}}(1,0)=\varnothing
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{1,m}}(0,1)=[m]
, then define for
n>1

\theta_{n,m}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\overbrace{\theta_{1,m}\coprod_{[0]}\theta_{1,m}\coprod_{[0]}...\coprod_{[0]}\theta_{1,m}}^{\text{$n$ times}},

where in each term of the iterated pushout
[0]
embeds as the final object of
\theta_{1,m}
on the left and the initial object of
\theta_{1,m}
on the right. More explicitly,
\theta_{n,m}
is a bicategory with
n
objects such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{n,m}}(i,j)
is empty unless
j\geq i
and in that case has objects given by sequences
(m_{1},m_{2},...,m_{j-i})
such that
0\leq m_{k}\leq m
with a unique morphism from
(m_{1},m_{2},...,m_{j-i})
to
(m^{\prime}_{1},m^{\prime}_{2},...,m^{\prime}_{j-i})
if all
m^{\prime}_{k}\geq m_{k}
. Also observe that we have an isomorphism

\theta_{n,m}\cong\overbrace{\theta_{n,1}\coprod_{[n]}\theta_{n,1}\coprod_{[n]}...\coprod_{[n]}\theta_{n,1}}^{\text{$m$ times}},

where the inclusions
[n]\hookrightarrow\theta_{n,1}
are identity on objects and send morphisms in
[n]
to sequences of the form
(1,1,...,1)
on the right and
(0,0,...,0)
on the left. Observe that there is a bicosimplicial object

\theta_{\bullet,\bullet}:\Delta\times\Delta\rightarrow 2-\mathrm{Cat}

sending
(i,j)
to
\theta_{i,j}
. Indeed, using the iterated pushouts above it suffices to only define the action of morphisms
f:e\rightarrow([n],[m])
where
e\in\{([0],[0]),([1],[0]),([1],[1])\}
. The action on
\theta_{0,0}
is obvious, to every
([1],[0])\xrightarrow{(ij,k)}([n],[m])
we assign a morphism
[1]\rightarrow\theta_{n,m}
sending the unique nontrivial morphism of
[1]
to
(k,k,...,k)
viewed as a morphism from
i
to
j
in
\theta_{n,m}
and finally we send every
([1],[1])\xrightarrow{(ij,st)}([n],[m])
to a morphism
\theta_{1,1}\rightarrow\theta_{n,m}
sending the unique non-trivial
2
-morphism of
\theta_{1,1}
to the
2
-morphism
(s,s,...,s)\rightarrow(t,t,...,t)
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{n,m}}(i,j)
.

Now given twofold Segal spaces
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
we define a bisimplicial space

\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{B})_{\bullet,\bullet}:([n],[m])\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{A}\times\theta_{n,m},\mathscr{B}),

it follows from the pushout isomorphisms above that it is indeed a twofold Segal space. Given
\alpha:\theta_{1,1}\times\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
such that
\alpha_{0}\cong F
and
\alpha_{1}\cong F^{\prime}
and
\beta:\theta_{1,1}\times\mathscr{B}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
such that
\beta_{0}\cong G
and
\beta_{1}\cong G^{\prime}
we define their composition to be

\theta_{1,1}\times\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{m}\theta_{2,1}\times\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\beta})}\mathscr{C},

where
m
is the morphism
\theta_{1,1}\xrightarrow{(02,01)}\theta_{2,1}
,
\widehat{\alpha}
is the composition

\theta_{1,1}\times\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathscr{B}\xrightarrow{G}\mathscr{C}

and
\widehat{\beta}
is

\theta_{1,1}\times\mathscr{A}\xrightarrow{F^{\prime}}\theta_{1,1}\times\mathscr{B}\xrightarrow{\beta}\mathscr{C}.

Notation 4.3
.

We will use
4.2
as the default tricategory structure on
2-\mathrm{Cat}
, however in some cases we will need a slightly different notion. To describe it, we will first define a bicategory
\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}
for objects
\theta_{l,m}
and
\theta_{t,n}
of
4.2
. The objects of
\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}
are given by pairs
(i,j)
where
0\leq i\leq t
and
0\leq j\leq l
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}}((i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\begin{cases}\varnothing&\text{ if $i^{\prime}<i$ or $j^{\prime}<j$}\\
\mathrm{Ar}_{\theta_{l,m}}^{i^{\prime}-i,\mathrm{lax}}(j,j^{\prime})&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{Ar}_{\theta_{l,m}}^{i^{\prime}-i,\mathrm{lax}}(j,j^{\prime})
is a category with objects given by pairs
(f,s)
, where
f:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
is a morphism in
\theta_{t,n}
and
s
is a sequence

j\xrightarrow{s_{1}}j_{1}\xrightarrow{s_{2}}...\xrightarrow{s_{i^{\prime}-i}}j_{i^{\prime}-i}\xrightarrow{s_{i^{\prime}-i+1}}j^{\prime}

in
\theta_{l,m}
. Observe that by definition every morphism from
j
to
j^{\prime}
in
\theta_{l,m}
is of the from
s_{j^{\prime}-j}\circ...\circ s_{1}
, where each
s_{k}
is a morphism from
j+k-1
to
j+k
. We can thus identify an object of
\mathrm{Ar}_{\theta_{l,m}}^{i^{\prime}-i,\mathrm{lax}}(j,j^{\prime})
with the data of a morphism
f:i\rightarrow i^{\prime}
in
\theta_{t,n}
, a morphism
s:j\rightarrow j^{\prime}
in
\theta_{l,m}
and
(i^{\prime}-i)
marked points
j\leq j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq...\leq j_{i^{\prime}-i}\leq j^{\prime}
in
[j^{\prime}-j]
. Using this description, we define the space of morphisms from
(f,s,j\leq j_{1}\leq...\leq j^{\prime})
to
(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{s},l\leq\widetilde{j_{1}}\leq...\leq j^{\prime})
to be empty unless
j_{k}\leq\widetilde{j_{k}}
for all
k
in which case it is given by

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{t,n}}(i,i^{\prime})}(f,f^{\prime})\times\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{l,m}}(j,j^{\prime})}(s,s^{\prime}).

The composition of

(f:i\rightarrow i^{\prime},s:j\rightarrow j^{\prime},j\leq j_{1}\leq...\leq j_{i^{\prime}-i}\leq j)

and

(f^{\prime}:i^{\prime}\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime}:j^{\prime}\rightarrow j^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime}\leq j^{\prime}_{1}\leq...\leq j^{\prime}_{i^{\prime\prime}-i^{\prime}}\leq j^{\prime\prime})

is given by

f^{\prime}\circ f:i\rightarrow i^{\prime\prime},s^{\prime}\circ s:j\rightarrow j^{\prime\prime},j\leq j_{1}\leq...\leq j_{i^{\prime}-i}\leq j^{\prime}_{1}\leq...\leq j^{\prime}_{i^{\prime\prime}-i^{\prime}}\leq j^{\prime\prime}.

It is obvious that every morphism
F:\theta_{l_{1},m_{1}}\rightarrow\theta_{l_{2},m_{2}}
induces morphisms
\mathrm{Ar}_{\theta_{l_{1},m_{1}}}^{i^{\prime}-i}(j,j^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}_{\theta_{l_{2},m_{2}}}^{i^{\prime}-i}(f(j),f(j^{\prime}))
, so we can consider
(\theta_{l_{1},m_{1}}\mapsto\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l_{1},m_{1}})
as a functor from
\Delta\times\Delta
to
\mathrm{Cat}_{2}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}\cong\theta_{l,m}\otimes\theta_{t,n}.

Proof.

The description of objects and morphisms of
\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}
is symmetric in
\theta_{l,m}
and
\theta_{t,n}
except possibly for the markings, so to prove the claim it suffices to establish for any
k,p\geq 0
a bijection between
p
-markings of
[k]
and
k
-markings of
[p]
. Assume we are given
0\leq a_{1}\leq...\leq a_{p}\leq k
, then for any
j\in[k]
define
b_{j}\in[p]
to be the maximal
q
for which
a_{q}<j
. We can apply the same construction to a
k
marking of
[p]
, the fact that they are inverse to each other amounts to the statement that
a_{r}
can be characterized as the maximal element
i
of
[k]
such that
a_{r-1}\leq i\leq a_{r}
which is obvious.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

There are natural isomorphisms

(2)

\displaystyle\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}\cong\overbrace{(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m})\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\{1\}}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m})\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\{2\}}...\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\{n-1\}}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m})}^{\text{$l$ times}}

and

(3)

\displaystyle\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m}\cong\overbrace{(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,1})\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes[1]}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,1})\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes[1]}...\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes[1]}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,1})}^{\text{$m$ times}}.

Proof.

First, observe that the sets of objects on both sides of
eq.

2
and
eq.

3
are isomorphic. An object of
\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m}
can be identified with a pair
(j,i)
with
0\leq j\leq t
and
i\in\{0,1\}
. A morphism from
(j,0)
to
(j,1)
is given by the data
(f,j\xrightarrow{s_{1}}j_{1}\xrightarrow{s_{2}}j^{\prime})
, where
f
is a morphism from 0 to 1 in
\theta_{1,m}
. It follows that a morphism from
(j,i)
to
(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})
in

\overbrace{(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m})\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\{1\}}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m})\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\{2\}}...\coprod_{\theta_{t,n}\otimes\{n-1\}}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m})}^{\text{$l$ times}}

can be identified with the following data:

(f:i\rightarrow i^{\prime},j\xrightarrow{s_{1}}j_{1}\xrightarrow{s_{2}}j_{2},j_{1}\xrightarrow{s_{2}}j_{2}\xrightarrow{s_{3}}j_{3},...,j_{i^{\prime}-i-1}\xrightarrow{s_{i^{\prime}-i}}j_{i^{\prime}-i}\xrightarrow{s_{i^{\prime}-i+1}}j^{\prime}),

which is obviously the same as the data needed to define a morphism in
\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m}
. Similarly, since every 2-morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{t,l}}(j,j^{\prime})
is of the form
\alpha_{j^{\prime}-j}*\alpha_{j^{\prime}-j-1}*...*\alpha_{1}
, where
\alpha_{k}
is a 2-morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\theta_{t,n}}(j+k-1,j+k)
, we see that the spaces of 2-morphisms are also isomorphic. Finally, the isomorphism of
eq.

3
follows easily from the description of
\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{1,m}
given above.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

For a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
and a pair of non-negative integers
(t,n)
we can consider a functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
given by

(l,m)\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\theta_{t,n}\otimes\theta_{l,m},\mathscr{B}).

It follows from
lemma

4.5
that this functor satisfies the Segal condition and so defines a twofold Segal space that we will denote
\mathrm{Ar}^{\theta_{t,n},\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
and simply
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
for
t=1
and
n=0
. Moreover, we can consider

(t,n)\mapsto\mathrm{Ar}^{\theta_{t,n},\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})

as a functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
and it once again follows from
lemma

4.4
and
lemma

4.5
that it satisfies the Segal condition. In particular, for any other twofold Segal space
\mathscr{E}
we can consider a functor

(n,m)\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{E},\mathrm{Ar}^{\theta_{n,m},\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B}))

and it will be a twofold Segal space as well. We denote it by
\mathrm{Hom}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{B})
.

Remark 4.7
.

The category
[n]\otimes[m]
is in fact the Gray tensor product of
[n]
and
[m]
viewed as
2
-categories. The
\infty
-categorical version of this construction can be found in
[
GR17
]
,
[
Hau20
]
and
[
GHL20b
]
.

Remark 4.8
.

More explicitly, an object of
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
is an arrow
f:a\rightarrow b
in
\mathscr{B}
, a morphism from
f
to
t
is given by a diagram

Definition 4.9
.

Given a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
and a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
, we define a
lax functor
from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{B}
to be a morphism from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{B}
considered as categories over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
that take coCartesian morphisms over inert morphisms in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
to coCartesian morphisms. We call a lax functor
unital
if it additionally takes coCartesian morphisms lying over surjective active morphisms to coCartesian morphisms. We denote by
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})
(resp. by
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})
) the space of lax functors (resp. unital lax functors) from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{B}
and denote a lax functor by
F:\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{B}
. We endow this space with the structure of a twofold Segal space by declaring a morphism from
F
to
G
to be a morphism
f:\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
such that its composition with the natural projection
p_{1}:\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
(resp. with
p_{2}:\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
) is isomorphic to
F
(resp. to
G
) and a 2-morphism
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
to be a morphism
\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathrm{Ar}^{\theta_{1,1},\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
whose natural projections to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B}))
are isomorphic to
f
and
g
respectively.

Construction 4.10
.

Given categories
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{C}
together with a subcategory
i:\mathscr{D}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}
such that
i_{0}:\mathscr{D}_{0}\overset{\sim}{\hookrightarrow}\mathscr{C}_{0}
is an isomorphism, denote by
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{E},\mathscr{C})
the category whose objects are functors
F:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
and whose morphisms are natural transformations
\alpha:F\rightarrow G
such that all
\alpha_{e}:F(e)\rightarrow G(e)
belong to
\mathscr{D}
, also denote by
\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C},n}(\mathscr{D})
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{C})
on those morphisms
[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
that factor through
\mathscr{D}
. Denote by
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
the Segal fibration over
\mathpzc{Cat}
sending
[n]
to
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathscr{D}}([n],\mathscr{C})
. We will also denote
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})}(\mathscr{S})
by
\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
.

Proposition 4.11
.

\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
is an extendable Segal pattern. Moreover, each intermediate subcategory inclusion
\mathscr{D}\overset{j}{\hookrightarrow}\mathscr{A}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}
induces an extendable Segal morphism
j:\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{C})
and the adjunction

L_{\mathrm{Seg}}j_{!}:\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{C}):j^{*}

is monadic.

Proof.

Observe that for every object
f\in\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
we have

(\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C}))^{\mathrm{el}}_{f/}\cong(\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{A}}(\mathscr{C}))^{\mathrm{el}}_{j(f)/}\cong(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}})^{\mathrm{el}}_{f/},

so the fact that
j
is a Segal morphism follows. The next two statements would follow from
[
CH19
, Proposition 9.5]
and
[
CH19
, Corollary 9.17]
respectively if we prove that
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}
is a Segal fibration. However, this reduces to an obvious isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathscr{D}}([n],\mathscr{C})\cong\underset{e\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[n]/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathscr{D}}(e,\mathscr{C}).

The last statement now follows from
[
CH19
, Proposition 8.1]
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

Observe that there are two natural subcategories of
\mathscr{C}
: the entire category
\mathscr{C}
and
\widetilde{i}:\mathscr{C}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}
. In the first case we will denote
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{C})
simply by
\mathpzc{coCart}(\mathscr{C})
. Observe that
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{C}^{\sim}}(\mathscr{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
. Moreover, for every subcategory
\mathscr{D}
the inclusion
\widetilde{i}
factors through
\mathscr{D}
, and so by
4.11
we have an induced morphism
L_{\mathrm{Seg}}\widetilde{i}_{\mathscr{D},!}:\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
and moreover
\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
is isomorphic to the category of algebras for the monad
\widetilde{i}^{*}_{\mathscr{D}}L_{\mathrm{Seg}}\widetilde{i}_{\mathscr{D},!}
.

Proposition 4.13
.

The monad
\widetilde{i}^{*}_{\mathscr{D}}L_{\mathrm{Seg}}\widetilde{i}_{\mathscr{D},!}
on
\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
is isomorphic to

L_{\mathrm{coCart}}^{\mathscr{D}}:(p:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\mapsto\mathscr{E}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{D})\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}.

Proof.

Observe that every morphism
f\xrightarrow{\alpha}g
in
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
decomposes uniquely as
f\xrightarrow{m}h\xrightarrow{\alpha_{\mathscr{D}}}g
where
m
is a morphism in
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
and
\alpha_{\mathscr{D}}
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathscr{D}}([m],\mathscr{C})
for some
m
- this follows since
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
is a coCartesian fibration over
\mathpzc{Cat}
. The statement now follows easily since by definition we have

\widetilde{i}^{*}_{\mathscr{D}}\widetilde{i}_{\mathscr{D},!}\mathscr{E}(f)\cong\underset{(\widetilde{i}f^{\prime}\rightarrow f)\in\widetilde{i}/f}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{E}(f^{\prime})\cong\underset{(f^{\prime}\rightarrow f)\in\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C},n}(\mathscr{D})}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{E}(f^{\prime})\cong\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}(f)

for any
(f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
, where the second isomorphism follows from the factorization described above. Since this is obviously a Segal
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
-space, we see that
L_{\mathrm{Seg}}\widetilde{i}_{\mathscr{D},!}\cong\widetilde{i}_{\mathscr{D},!}
.
∎

Notation 4.14
.

We will often identify
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
with the underlying overcategory
p:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
. Observe that if we have
a\in\mathscr{F}_{x}
and a morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
belonging to the subcategory
\mathscr{D}
, then we have a morphism
f_{*}:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
and so a corresponding functor
f_{*}:\mathscr{F}_{x}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}_{y}
. Moreover, we can view
f
as a morphism from
x
to
f:[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
in
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
, so we also have a functor
\mathrm{Ob}(\mathscr{F}_{x})\rightarrow\mathscr{F}_{f}
. We will call the arrows in the essential image of this functor
cocartesian
and denote them by
f_{*}
; an arrow of this form has
a\in\mathscr{F}_{x}
as the source and
f_{*}(a)\in\mathscr{F}_{y}
as the target.

Notation 4.15
.

We denote by
\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}
the full subbicategory of
\mathrm{Cat}
on
\varnothing
and
[n]
for
n\geq 0
. Observe that the underlying category of
\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}
is
\Delta_{a}
, in particular there is a natural inclusion
i:\Delta_{a}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}
. Observe that it also inherits the monoidal structure from
\Delta_{a}
given by

([n],[m])\mapsto[m+n+1]

with the unit object given by
\varnothing
. In particular, we can consider the tricategory
\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}
. Now let
\mathscr{T}
be an arbitrary tricategory. Recall from
4.31
that giving a monad in
\mathscr{T}
is equivalent to giving a functor
T:\mathrm{B}\Delta_{a}\rightarrow\mathscr{T}
. We will call the monad
T

lax-idempotent
if factors through
i:\mathrm{B}\Delta_{a}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{B}\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

A monad
T
admits a lax idempotent structure if and only if the unit morphism
eT:T\rightarrow TT
is right adjoint to multiplication
m:TT\rightarrow T
, in which case this structure is unique.

Proof.

First, observe that a morphism
f:[m]\rightarrow[n]
in
\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}
is a right adjoint if it preserves the maximal element and a left adjoint if it preserves the minimal element. In particular, if there is a lax idempotent structure on a monad
T
then we certainly have the adjunction described in the claim.

Conversely, denote by
\theta:i_{0}\rightarrow i_{1}
the natural transformation between the two inert morphisms
i_{0,1}:[0]\rightarrow[1]
. We claim that a lax-idempotent structure is uniquely defined by the image of
\theta
. Indeed, it is easy to see that any natural transformation
\alpha:f\rightarrow g
where
f
and
g
are functors from
[m]
to
[n]
has the form
\theta^{g(0)-f(0)}\otimes\theta^{g(1)-f(1)}\otimes...\otimes\theta^{g(m)-f(m)}
. Now observe that
\theta
itself is isomorphic to the composite

i_{0}\cong\mathrm{id}\circ i_{0}\xrightarrow{\eta}i_{1}\circ p\circ i_{0}\cong i_{1},

where
p:[1]\rightarrow[0]
denotes the unique morphism and
\eta
is the unit of the adjunction
p\dashv i_{1}
. It follows that the structure of the lax-idempotent monad is uniquely determined by the data of the adjunction
m\dashv eT
and this data is unique if it exists.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

Given a lax-idempotent monad on a flagged bicategory
\mathscr{B}
and an object
b\in\mathscr{B}
, there is a structure of a
T
-algebra on
b
if and only if the unit morphism
e:b\rightarrow Tb
admits a left adjoint
t
such that
t\circ b\cong\mathrm{id}
, and this structure is unique if it exists.

Proof.

Consider a functor
F:\Delta_{a}\cong(\Delta^{\mathrm{act}})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
that sends
[n]
to
T^{n}b
and every morphism to the corresponding structural morphism of the monad
T
. It follows from
lemma

3.22
that giving
b
a structure of a
T
-algebra is equivalent to extending this to a functor
\widetilde{F}\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}_{-\infty}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
. First assume that
e:b\rightarrow Tb
has a left adjoint
t:Tb\rightarrow b
. We claim that
t
gives
b
the structure of a
T
-algebra. Observe that every inert morphism in
\Delta_{-\infty}
is a composition of
s_{\infty,n};[n]\hookrightarrow[n+1]
that maps
[n]
isomorphically onto the initial segment of
[n+1]
. We declare
\widetilde{F}(s_{\infty,n})
to be
T^{n+1}b\xrightarrow{t}T^{n}b
. We now need to check that for every factorization square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\infty,n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

in
\Delta_{-\infty}
we have
\widetilde{F}(s_{\infty,n})\circ F(a)\cong F(\widetilde{a})\circ\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{s})
. Now observe that if
a
is such that its restriction to
[n,n+1]
is equal to identity, then the commutativity is trivial. Using the equivalence

\mathrm{Act}{Cat}([n])\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[n]/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Act}(e)

we are thus reduced to the case
n=0
. We are then reduced to proving the commutativity of the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{45.21855pt}{-2.15276pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {
