{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -37.94 -36.12 L 43.19 -36.12}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -37.94 -36.12 L 43.19 -36.12}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The first of these diagrams commute by assumption, to prove he commutativity of the second diagram, observe that every morphism in it has a right adjoint (by the definition of
t
and since
T
is lax-idempotent), so it suffices to prove the commutativity of the corresponding diagram of right adjoints. It is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tb}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{m}b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{m-1}}

and it obviously commutes.

Now assume that
b
has a
T
-algebra structure
t:Tb\rightarrow b
. The isomorphism
t\circ e
then follows from the definition. Moreover, it is elementary to see that the composition

\mathrm{id}_{Tb}\cong Tt\circ Te\xrightarrow{\eta_{m}}Tt\circ eT\circ m\circ Te\cong Tt\circ eT\cong e\circ t,

where
\eta_{m}:\mathrm{id}_{T^{2}b}\rightarrow eT\circ m
is the unit of the adjunction
m\dashv eT
provides a unit for the adjunction
t\dashv e
. It follows that the structure of a
T
-algebra is necessarily given by the left adjoint to
e:b\rightarrow Tb
, and this morphism is unique if it exists, which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Lax-idempotent monads (also frequently called
KZ-monads
) were introduced for ordinary categories in
[
Koc72
]
and
[
Zöb76
]
, a significantly more detailed account of them in classical terms can be found in
[
BF06
]
.

Proposition 4.19
.

The structure of a
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibration on a given
(p:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{\mathscr{C}}
exists if and only if the natural inclusion
e:\mathscr{E}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{D})
admits a left adjoint.

Proof.

We will consider
\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
as a flagged bicategory. The category of
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibrations over
\mathscr{C}
is then isomorphic to the category of algebras for the monad that sends
p:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}
. Observe that
e:\mathscr{E}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{E}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{D})
is the unit morphism for the free
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibration monad. The morphism
e
is easily seen to be fully faithful: indeed, it sends an object
x\in\mathscr{E}
to the pair
(x,\mathrm{id}_{p(x)})
and it follows directly from the definitions that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}}((x,\mathrm{id}_{p(x)}),(y,\mathrm{id}_{p(y)}))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{E}}(x,y).

Now observe that this monad is lax-idempotent: indeed, by definition
T^{n}_{\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})}(\mathscr{E}\xrightarrow{p}\mathscr{C})\cong\mathscr{E}\times_{\mathscr{C}}\mathrm{Ar}^{n}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{D})
; since
\mathrm{Ar}_{\mathscr{C}}(\mathscr{D})
is defined as a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{C})
, we see that this functor indeed extends to a functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{l.i.}}_{a}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathrm{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}})
.

By
4.17
the only thing we need to show is that for any left adjoint
m:\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}\rightarrow p
we have
m\circ e\cong\mathrm{id}_{p}
. Indeed, for any pair of objects
(x,y)
of
\mathscr{F}
we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(m\circ ex,y)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}}(ex,ey))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(x,y),

where the first isomorphism follows from the definition and the second follows since
e
is fully faithful, and so the claim follows by Yoneda lemma.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

Given a
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibration
p:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, the unit morphism
e:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{/p}
is given by sending
x
to
(x,\mathrm{id}_{x})\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{/p}
. The left adjoint
m:\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{/p}\rightarrow\mathscr{F}
sends
(x,f:p(x)\rightarrow y)
to
f_{*}x\in\mathscr{F}_{y}
. Observe that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{/p}}((x,f),e(z))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(y,p(z))\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(p(x),p(z))}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(x,z).

The universal property of left adjoint then gives

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(f_{*}x,z)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(y,p(z))\times_{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(p(x),p(z))}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(x,z).

Corollary 4.21
.

Given a
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibration
p:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, a morphism
\widetilde{f}:x\rightarrow y
in
\mathscr{F}
lying over
f:p(x)\rightarrow p(y)
in
\mathscr{D}
is coCartesian in the sense of
4.14
if and only if for any object
z\in\mathscr{F}
the following commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(y,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{F}}(x,z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(p(y),p(z))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(p(x),p(z))}

is a pullback square. Moreover,
p
is a
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibration if and only if every
\mathscr{D}
-morphism of
\mathscr{C}
admits a coCartesian lift.

Proof.

The claimed universal property is simply a restatement of
remark

4.20
. Assuming every morphism
f
of
\mathscr{C}
admits a coCartesian lift, we can define a left adjoint to
e:p\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\mathscr{D}}_{p/}
by sending
(x,f:px\rightarrow y)
to the target of the coCartesian lift of
f
.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

4.21
and
[
Lur09
, Proposition 2.4.4.3.]
allows us to identify the category of
\mathscr{D}
-coCartesian fibrations in the sense of
4.11
and the category with objects
p:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
for which all morphisms in
\mathscr{D}
admit
p
=coCartesian lifts and with morphisms given by those morphisms over
\mathscr{C}
that preserve those lifts. In the case
\mathscr{D}\cong\mathscr{C}
we can alternatively conclude by observing the isomorphism between
L^{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathrm{coCart}}
and the free fibration monad of
[
GHN15
]
.

Proposition 4.23
.

Given a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
, there exists a twofold Segal space
L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
such that for every twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}

\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B}).

Proof.

First, observe that it is enough to present an isomorphism on the level of underlying spaces, since the isomorphism on morphisms and 2-morphisms would then follow from the same argument applied to
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
and
\mathrm{Ar}^{\theta_{1,1},\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
. We begin by defining

L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathscr{C}\times_{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{act}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}),

where
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{act}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
is a coCartesian fibration over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\cong\mathpzc{Cat}
corresponding to a functor that sends
s\in\mathpzc{Cat}
to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act}}_{/s}
.

To prove that this object satisfies the required universal property, first observe that a morphism of twofold Segal spaces can be identified with a morphism of the corresponding coCartesian fibrations over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
, while a lax functor can be identified with a morphism in
\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
. Denote by
i^{\mathrm{int}}:(\Delta^{\mathrm{int}})^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
the natural inclusion, then by
4.11
the natural forgetful functor
U:\mathrm{coCart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})\rightarrow\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
admits a left adjoint
L_{\mathrm{Seg}}i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}
. We will prove that
i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C}\cong L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
, in particular it would follow that it satisfies the
\mathpzc{coCart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
Segal condition, so in particular
L_{\mathrm{Seg}}i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C}\cong i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C}
. For any morphism
f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
we have by definition

i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C}(f)\cong\underset{(g\rightarrow f)\in(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}(g)\cong\underset{(g\rightarrow f)\in(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}(g(0)),

where
g:[n]\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
is a functor, the isomorphism
\mathscr{C}(g)\cong\mathscr{C}(g(0))
follows since
\mathscr{C}
is a right fibration over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
and
(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}
is a category whose objects are morphisms commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}

in
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
and morphisms
i:g\rightarrow h
are given by diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}

where for all
j
we have
t_{j}\circ i_{j}\cong s_{j}
and all
i_{j}
are inert.

Denote by
(\mathrm{Act}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))_{/f}
the groupoid whose objects are objects
s:g\rightarrow f
of
(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}
as above for which all
s_{j}
are active morphisms. Observe that
(\mathrm{Act}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))_{/f}
is a full subcategory of
(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}
and also that we have an isomorphism

L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}(f)\cong\underset{(g\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}f)\in(\mathrm{Act}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))_{/f}}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{C}(g(0)).

We will now prove that the inclusion
(\mathrm{Act}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))_{/f}\hookrightarrow(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}
is cofinal, it would imply that
i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C}\cong L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
using the isomorphism above. In fact, for every object
(g\xrightarrow{s}f)\in(\mathrm{Ar}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))^{\mathrm{int}}_{/f}
we will produce a unique up to isomorphism morphism to an object of
(\mathrm{Act}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))_{/f}
. To do this first denote by
f(k-1)\overset{f^{i}_{k}}{\rightarrowtail}c_{k}\overset{f^{a}_{k}}{\twoheadrightarrow}f(k)
the active/inert factorization of
f_{k}
and introduce similar notation for
g_{k}
and
s_{k}
, then observe that we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(n-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{i}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{i}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{a}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{i}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{i}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{i}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{i}_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{a}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{i}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{a}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{a}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{a}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{a}_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{a}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{a}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{i}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{a}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{i}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{i}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{a}_{n}}

in which every square is given by active/inert factorization. The fact that
b_{k}
that appears in the active/inert factorization of both
s^{i}_{k}\circ g^{a}_{k}
and
f^{i}_{k}\circ s^{a}_{k}
is the same object follows since
(f^{a}_{k}\circ v_{k})\circ(h^{i}_{k}\circ s^{i}_{k-1})
and
(s_{k}^{a}\circ h^{a}_{k})\circ(u_{i}\circ g^{i}_{k})
represent the active/inert factorization of the same morphism. The object
(h\overset{s_{1}}{\twoheadrightarrow}f)
obviously belongs to
(\mathrm{Act}^{n}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}))_{/f}
and the morphism
(g\overset{s_{i}}{\rightarrowtail}h)
is unique by the uniqueness of the active/inert factorization.

It follows that
i^{\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C}\cong L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
. It remains to prove that this is indeed a twofold Segal space. For this we need to show that both
\mathrm{Act}{Cat}([n])
and
\mathrm{Act}^{2}{Cat}([n])
are Segal spaces, which easily follows since
\mathpzc{Cat}
is extendable; this proves that
L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}
is a double Segal space. Moreover, since there are no non-trivial active morphisms with target
[0]
, we also see that
L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}([0])\cong\mathscr{C}([0])
is a groupoid, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

Morphisms of the form
i\circ s
where
i
is an inert morphism and
s
is a surjective active morphism form a subcategory of
\Delta
.

Proof.

It would suffice to prove that if

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

is a factorization square in
\Delta
in which
i
is inert and
t
is surjective, then
s
is also surjective. However, this is obvious:
i
is an inclusion of a subinterval,
s
is the restriction of
t
to that subinterval and
j
is the inclusion of the image of
x
under
t
, so
s
is obviously surjective.
∎

Notation 4.25
.

We will denote by
\Delta^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}
the subcategory described in
lemma

4.24
.

Corollary 4.26
.

Given a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
there exists a twofold Segal space
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}\mathscr{C}
such that for every two-fold Segal space
\mathscr{B}

\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{2-\mathrm{Cat}}(L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B}).

Proof.

First, observe that just like in
4.23
it suffices to prove the statement at the level of underlying spaces. A unital lax functor is equivalently a morphism in
\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
and we know by
4.11
that the natural forgetful functor
U:\mathrm{coCart}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})\rightarrow\mathrm{coCart}^{sur,\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
admits a left adjoint
L_{\mathrm{Seg}}i^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}_{!}
. It is
a priori
not a twofold Segal space, but it is at least a presheaf on
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
, so it factors through a universal twofold Segal space
L_{\mathrm{2-Seg}}(L_{\mathrm{Seg}}i^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C})
and we define
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}\mathscr{C}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}L_{\mathrm{2-Seg}}(L_{\mathrm{Seg}}i^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}_{!}\mathscr{C})
.
∎

Lemma 4.27
.

Given an injective morphism
i:[l]\hookrightarrow[n]
and a morphism
s:[m]\rightarrow[n]
in
\Delta
such that
\mathrm{Im}(s)\cap\mathrm{Im}(i)\neq\varnothing
we have that the
x
in the following pullback square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[l]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

in
\mathrm{Cat}
is isomorphic to an object of
\Delta
.

Proof.

Indeed, the set of objects of
x
is obviously finite and nonempty, its fiber over every
\{j\}\in[l]
is a (possibly empty) linearly ordered set. Moreover, if
\{j<j^{\prime}\}\in[l]
and the corresponding fibers are nonempty, then there is a single morphism from any element of
x_{j}
to the smallest element of
x_{j^{\prime}}
. It follows that
x
is indeed a finite linearly ordered set.
∎

Construction 4.28
.

Define the bicategory
\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}
for
n\geq 0
as follows: first define its objects to be natural numbers
0\leq i\leq n
. define
\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}}(i)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\Delta_{a}
. Define
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}}(i,j)\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\varnothing
if
i>j
. To define
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}}(i,j)
in the other cases first consider the category
(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}
whose objects are active injective morphisms
[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j-i]
. Consider a functor
F_{i,j}:(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}
that sends
[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j-i]
to
\Delta^{n}_{a}
and sends an injective morphism
i:[m]\hookrightarrow[n]
to the natural projection
i^{*}:\Delta^{n}_{a}\rightarrow\Delta^{m}_{a}
. Finally, define
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}}(i,j)
to be the total category corresponding to
F_{i,j}
if
j>i
. Given two composable morphisms
(a:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j-i],c\in\Delta^{n}_{a})
and
(b:[m]\twoheadrightarrow[k-j],d\in\Delta^{m}_{a})
, define their composition to be a pair
(a+b:[m+n]\twoheadrightarrow[k-i],c+d\in\Delta^{(m+n)}_{a})
where
(a+b)
is an injective active morphism obtained by gluing
a
and
b
and
(c+d)
has the same projections to the first
(j-i-1)
components as
c
and the same projection to the last
(k-j-1)
components as
d
and whose projection to the
(j-i)
-th component is given by the sum of the corresponding components of
c
and
d
.

Lemma 4.29
.

There is a natural equivalence

L^{\mathrm{lax}}[n]\cong\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}

Proof.

By applying
4.23
we see that the objects of
L^{\mathrm{lax}}[n]
are in bijection with the objects of
[n]
. Given a pair
i\leq j
, denote by
f_{i,j}:[1]\rightarrow[j-i]
the unique active morphism in
\Delta
, then it is easy to see that the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{L^{\mathrm{lax}}[n]}(i,j)
is isomorphic to
(\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{op}}_{f_{i,j}/}\cong(\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}
.

We first prove the claim in the special case
i=j
. The claim is then reduced to

\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}}\cong\Delta_{a}

To prove it recall the classical fact that
\Delta
is generated by morphisms
\delta^{n}_{i}:[n-1]\rightarrow[n]
and
\sigma^{n}_{i}:[n+1]\rightarrow[n]
for
0\leq i\leq n
that satisfy

\displaystyle\delta^{n+1}_{i}\circ\delta^{n}_{j}\cong\delta^{n+1}_{j+1}\circ\delta^{n}_{i}\;\text{ if }i\leq j

\displaystyle\sigma^{n}_{j}\circ\sigma^{n+1}_{i}\cong\sigma^{n}_{i}\circ\sigma^{n+1}_{j+1}\;\text{ if }i\leq j

\displaystyle\sigma^{n}_{j}\circ\delta^{n+1}_{i}\cong\begin{cases}\delta^{n}_{i}\circ\sigma^{n-1}_{j-1}&\text{ if }i<j\\
\mathrm{id}_{[n]}\;\text{ if }i=j&\text{ or }i=j+1\\
\delta^{n}_{i-1}\circ\sigma^{n-1}_{j}&\text{ if }j+1<i\\
\end{cases}.

Out of these morphisms only
\delta^{n}_{0}
and
\delta^{n}_{n}
are inert and the rest are active. We define a morphism
F:\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\Delta_{a}
by sending
[n]
to
[n-1]
, sending
\delta^{n}_{o<i<n}:[n-1]\rightarrow[n]
to
\sigma^{n-2}_{i-1}:[n-1]\rightarrow[n-2]
and sending
\sigma^{n}_{i}:[n+1]\rightarrow[n]
to
\delta^{n}_{i}:[n-1]\rightarrow[n]
. It is elementary to check that this map preserves the necessary relations, so since it induces an isomorphism on the set of generators we see that
F
is indeed an isomorphism.

Now assume
j>i
. In this case first observe that any active morphism
a:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j-i]
in
\Delta
decomposes as
a\cong t\circ s
where
t
is injective and
s
is surjective. In particular, there is a functor
F:(\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}
sending each active morphism to its the injective component. Given an active injective morphism
t:[m]\hookrightarrow[j-i]
, it is easy to see that the fiber of
F
over
t
is isomorphic to
(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{op}})^{m}\cong\Delta_{a}^{m}
. Moreover, given a morphism
q:t\rightarrow t^{\prime}
observe that we can form the following diagram in
\Delta

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

where the top square is a pullback (which exists by
lemma

4.27
). Using the universal property of the pullback we see that
F:(\Delta^{\mathrm{act}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}
is a coCartesian fibration. Moreover, it is easy to see that under the isomorphism
F_{t}\cong\Delta^{m}_{a}
and
F_{t^{\prime}}\cong\Delta^{m^{\prime}}_{a}
the functor
q_{*}:F_{t}\rightarrow F_{t^{\prime}}
is exactly the same as described in
4.28
.
∎

Corollary 4.30
.

The bicategory
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}[n]
has objects given by natural numbers
0\leq i\leq n
and the morphisms categories are given by
\mathrm{Hom}_{L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}}(i,j)\cong(\Delta^{\mathrm{act},\mathrm{inj}}_{/[j-i]})^{\mathrm{op}}
with composition given by gluing active morphisms.

Proof.

Observe that by definition a unital lax functor
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
is a lax functor
F:\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
that sends all surjective active morphisms
s:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[j-i]
to identities. By examining the construction of
\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}
and the proof of
lemma

4.29
, we see that those are functors
F
sending all endomorphisms in
\mathrm{End}_{\mathrm{Bimod}_{n}}(i)\cong\Delta_{a}
for all
i
to identities. It now follows easily that the space of those functors is indeed corepresented by the category described in the claim.
∎

Corollary 4.31
.

Giving a lax functor from a point to a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
is equivalent to giving a monad in
\mathscr{B}
. In particular, giving a monad in
\mathscr{B}
is equivalent to giving a morphism

\mathrm{B}\Delta_{a}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}.

Proof.

Indeed, for a given object
b\in\mathscr{B}
giving a monad with the underlying object
b
is by definition equivalent to giving an algebra in the monoidal category
\mathrm{End}_{\mathscr{B}}(b)
. If we view
\mathrm{End}_{\mathscr{B}}(b)
as a coCartesian fibration over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
, then this in turn is equivalent to giving a morphism
*\rightarrow\mathrm{End}_{\mathscr{B}}(b)
over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
that sends coCartesian morphisms lying over inert morphisms to coCartesian morphisms. This in turn is by definition the same thing as a lax functor
*\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{B}
sending
*
to
b
.

The second statement now follows from
lemma

4.29
and the description of
\mathrm{Bimod}_{0}
.
∎

Construction 4.32
.

Observe that since
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
is a coCartesian fibration over
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}
we have that every morphism in
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
canonically decomposes as
a\circ s
where
a:([n]\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{C})\rightarrow([m]\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}\mathscr{C})
is a morphism of categories over
\mathscr{C}
and
s
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathscr{D}}([m],\mathscr{C})
. Denote by

p:\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\mathscr{C}

the morphism that sends
(c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}...\xrightarrow{f_{n}}c_{n})
to
c_{0}
, sends every morphism
s
given by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{c_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}

in which all
s_{i}
belong to
\mathscr{D}
to
s_{0}:c_{0}\rightarrow\overline{c_{0}}
, sends every active morphism in
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
to the identity and sends every inert morphism of the form

(c_{k}\xrightarrow{f_{k+1}}c_{k+1}\xrightarrow{f_{k+2}}...\xrightarrow{f_{l}}c_{l})\subset(c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}...\xrightarrow{f_{n}}c_{n})

to

(f_{k}\circ f_{k-1}\circ...\circ f_{1}):c_{0}\rightarrow c_{k}.

Lemma 4.33
.

The morphism

p^{*}:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C}),\mathscr{S})

restricts to a morphism

p^{*}:\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C}).

Proof.

Denote by
\mathscr{F}
an arbitrary presheaf on
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}
, then we need to prove that
p^{*}\mathscr{F}
satisfies the Segal condition. The objects of
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathscr{D}}(\mathscr{C})
are given by morphisms
f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, so we will prove the claim by induction on
n
, the case
n=0
being obvious. Now let
f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
be given by
(c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}...\xrightarrow{f_{n}}c_{n})
and assume that we have proved the claims for all chains of length
\leq n-1
, then we have the following isomorphisms

\displaystyle p^{*}\mathscr{F}(f)\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(c_{0})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(c_{0})\times_{\mathscr{F}(c_{1})}\mathscr{F}(c_{1})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{1})\times_{p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{1})}p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}...\xrightarrow{f_{n}}c_{n})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}c_{1})\times_{p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{1})}p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}c_{2})\times_{p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{2})}...\times_{p^{*}\mathscr{F}(c_{n-1})}\mathscr{F}(c_{n-1}\xrightarrow{f_{n}}c_{n}),

where the last isomorphism uses the inductive hypothesis. This concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Proposition 4.34
.

For any Segal space
\mathscr{C}
and a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{B}).

Similarly, there is a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{B}).

Proof.

The proofs of both claims are very similar and we will only prove the second one. First, observe that it would suffice to prove the statement for the underlying space on both sides, since the isomorphism on morphisms would then follow by applying the same argument to
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
.

To prove the claim, begin by observing that
\mathscr{C}
, when viewed as an object of
\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})
, corresponds to the presheaf
p^{*}\mathscr{C}
on
\mathpzc{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
in the notation of
lemma

4.33
. The claim now follows from the following sequence of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})}(p^{*}\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})}(p^{*}(\underset{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathscr{C}_{n}\otimes h_{[n]}),\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})}(\underset{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathrm{colim}}\;\mathscr{C}_{n}\otimes p^{*}h_{[n]},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}}{\lim}\;\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})}(\mathscr{C}_{n}\otimes h_{[n]},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{coCart}^{\mathrm{surj},\mathrm{int}}(\Delta^{\mathrm{op}})}(h_{[n]},\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\underset{(f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}([n],\mathscr{B}).

∎

Remark 4.35
.

Combining
4.30
with
4.34
we obtain an explicit description of
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}\mathscr{C}
for an arbitrary Segal space
\mathscr{C}
.

Notation 4.36
.

Observe that there is a natural inclusion
L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}[n]\hookrightarrow L^{\mathrm{lax}}[n]
which is identity on objects and sends
(f:[n]\rightarrow[j-i])\in\mathrm{Hom}_{L^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}}(i,j)
to
(f,(\varnothing,\varnothing,...,\varnothing)\in\Delta_{a}^{n})\in\mathrm{Hom}_{L^{\mathrm{lax}}}(i,j)
. Moreover, those morphisms are functorial with respect to morphisms in
\Delta
, so in particular using
4.34
we obtain for any twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
a functor

(-)^{\mathrm{unit}}:\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{B})\rightarrow\underset{([n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{B})\cong\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{unit}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{B})

which we will call the
unitalization
functor.

Definition 4.37
.

For a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
, a lax functor
F:\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{B}
and an object
b\in\mathscr{B}
we will define
lax cocone
of
F
over
b
to be a lax functor
G_{F}:\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright}\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{B}
such that
G_{F}|_{\mathscr{C}}\cong F
and the composition
*\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright}\overset{G_{F}}{\rightsquigarrow}\mathscr{B}
is the constant functor with the value
b
(where
*
denotes the vertex of the cone). We will endow the space of lax cocones over
b
with the structure of a Segal space by declaring a morphism
\alpha:G_{F}\rightarrow H_{F}
to be a natural transformation between
G_{F}
and
H_{F}
in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright},\mathscr{B})
such that the morphisms
\alpha_{c}
for
c\in\mathscr{C}
and
\alpha_{*}
are all identities. We will denote this Segal space by
\mathrm{coCone}_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(F,b)
. We will call
\widetilde{b}\in\mathscr{B}
a
lax colimit
of
F
and write

b\cong\underset{c\in\mathscr{C}}{\mathrm{colim}^{\mathrm{lax}}}F(c)

if
\mathrm{coCone}^{\mathrm{lax}}(F,b)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{B}}(\widetilde{b},b)
. We define lax cones and lax limits to be the lax cocones and lax colimits in
\mathscr{B}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Proposition 4.38
.

There is a natural equivalence

\mathrm{coCone}_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(F,b)\cong\underset{f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}}{\lim}\mathrm{coCone}_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(F\circ f,b).

Proof.

First, observe that just like in the proof of
4.34
it is enough to prove the statement for the underlying space of objects. Now recall the equivalence

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright},\mathscr{B})\cong\underset{f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}([n],\mathscr{B})

from the same proposition. By
[
Lur09
, Corollary 3.3.3.4.]
this limit is isomorphic to the space of coCartesian sections
s
of the left fibration
p:\mathscr{F}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright}}
sending
(f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\triangleright})
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}([n],\mathscr{B})
. Observe that the space
\mathrm{coCone}_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(F,b)
is isomorphic to the space of those sections that send
f
to
\{F\circ f\}
if
f([n])\subset\mathscr{C}
and for which
s(f)
factors through
\mathrm{coCone}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathscr{B}}(F\circ f,b)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}^{\mathrm{lax}}([n],\mathscr{B})
if
f(n)=*
. The required isomorphism easily follows.
∎

Remark 4.39
.

It follows from the definitions that for a lax functor
*\overset{T}{\rightsquigarrow}\mathscr{B}
(which is equivalently a monad by
4.31
) and an object
b
the category
\mathrm{coCone}_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(T,b)
is equivalent to the category of
T
-algebras in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{B}}(T(*),b)
. Indeed, denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\triangleright}_{/[1]}
the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[1]}
on those
g:[n+1]\rightarrow[1]
for which
g^{-1}(\{1\})\cong\{n+1\}
. Then by definition giving an object of
\mathrm{coCone}_{\mathscr{B}}^{\mathrm{lax}}(T,b)
is equivalent to giving a morphism
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\triangleright}_{/[1]}
that send
g
as above to
T^{n}f
for some
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{B}}(T(*),b)
. It is easy to see that
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\triangleright}_{/[1]}\cong(\Delta_{-\infty})^{\mathrm{op}}
. Using this equivalence and the structure of a monad on
T
, it is easy to see that functors like this can be equivalently defined as sections
s:\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{B}}(T(*),b)^{T}_{f}
satisfying the conditions described in the proof of
3.23
and those correspond to the
T
-algebra structures on
f
.

Construction 4.40
.

For an integer
n\geq 0
denote by
[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]
the category whose objects are pairs
(i,t)
where
0\leq i\leq n
and
t\in\{0,1\}
and such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]}((i,0),(i^{\prime},0))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]}(i,i^{\prime})
,
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]}((j,1),(j^{\prime},1))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]}(j^{\prime},j)
, the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]}((i,1),(j,0))
is empty and the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]}((i,0),(j,1))
has object given by cospans

and morphisms given by diagrams

The construction is obviously functorial in
[n]
, and so for any twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
we can define a functor
\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
sending
[n]
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n],\mathscr{B})
which is easily seen to be a twofold Segal space. We will call the resulting Segal space the
lax twisted arrow category
and denote it by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
.

Lemma 4.41
.

For a twofold Segal space
\mathscr{B}
denote by
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{radj}}(\mathscr{B})
(resp.
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{B})
) the subcategory of
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
with the same objects but with morphisms given by
[1]\otimes[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
that send all morphisms in
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\otimes[n]}((i,0),(j,0))
and in
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\otimes[n]}((i^{\prime},1),(j^{\prime},1))
to right (resp. left) adjoints and similarly denote by
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{B})
(resp.
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{radj}}(\mathscr{B})
) the subcategory of
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathscr{B})
with the same objects but with morphisms given by
[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]
that send the morphism in
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]}((i,0),(j,0))
to right (resp. left) adjoints and morphisms in
\mathrm{Hom}_{[1]\widetilde{\otimes}[n]}((i^{\prime},1),(j^{\prime},1))
to left (resp. right) adjoints. Then there are isomorphisms

\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{radj}}(\mathscr{B})\cong\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{B})

and

\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{B})\cong\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{radj}}(\mathscr{B}).

Proof.

We will only prove the first isomorphism, since the proof of the second is completely analogous. For every
n\geq 0
denote by
[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}
(resp.
[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}
) the category obtained by adding all right (resp. left) adjoints to
[n]
. Observe that there is an isomorphism
\mathscr{I}:[n]^{\mathrm{ladj},\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\sim}[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}
that sends every arrow in
[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}
to its right adjoint. By definition, all morphisms
[n]\otimes[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
in
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{radj}}(\mathscr{B})_{n}
factor through
[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}\otimes[1]
, where
[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}\otimes[1]
is the category with objects
(i,t)
where
i\in[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}
and
t\in\{0,1\}
such that

\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}\otimes[1]}((i,t),(j,t))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}}(i,j)

for
t=0
and
t=1
, the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}\otimes[1]}((i,1),(j,0))
is empty and
\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}\otimes[1]}((i,0),(j,1))
has objects given by strings
i\xrightarrow{f}k\xrightarrow{g}j
in
[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}
and morphisms by diagrams

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

in
[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}
.

Similarly, every morphism
[n]\widetilde{\otimes}[1]\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
in
\mathrm{TwAr}^{\mathrm{lax},\mathrm{ladj}}(\mathscr{B})_{n}
factor through
[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]
, where
[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]
is the category with objects
(i,t)
where
i\in[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}
and
t\in\{0,1\}
such that
\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]}((i,0),(j,0))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}}(i,j)
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]}((i,1),(j,1))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}}(j,i)
, the category
\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]}((i,1),(j,0))
is empty and
\mathrm{Hom}_{[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]}((i,0),(j,1))
has objects given by cospans
i\xrightarrow{s}l\xleftarrow{t}j
in
[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}
and morphisms by diagrams

(5)

}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{38.0946pt}{-8.83386pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

To finish the proof of the lemma, we will provide an isomorphism
\widetilde{\mathscr{I}}:[n]^{\mathrm{ladj}}\widetilde{\otimes}[1]\xrightarrow{\sim}[n]^{\mathrm{radj}}\otimes[1]
. The restriction of
\widetilde{\mathscr{I}}
to the fiber over
0
is given by the identity morphism, restriction to the fiber over
1
is given by
\mathscr{I}
. Finally, a morphism
i\xrightarrow{s}l\xleftarrow{t}j
is sent to
i\xrightarrow{s}l\xrightarrow{t^{R}}j
where
t^{R}
denotes the left adjoint. The 2-morphism represented by the diagram
5
is then sent to

(v\circ s\xrightarrow{\gamma}s^{\prime},t^{R}\xrightarrow{\eta}t^{\prime R}\circ t^{\prime}\circ t^{R}\xrightarrow{\delta}t^{\prime R}\circ v\circ t\circ t^{R}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}t^{\prime R}\circ v).

The inverse
\widetilde{\mathscr{I}}^{-1}
restricts to
\mathrm{id}
on the fiber over
0
, to
\mathscr{I}^{-1}
on the fiber over
1
and sends diagram
4
to

(h\circ f\xrightarrow{\alpha}f^{\prime},g^{\prime L}\xrightarrow{\eta}g^{\prime L}\circ g\circ g^{L}\xrightarrow{\beta}g^{\prime L}\circ g^{\prime}\circ h\circ g^{L}\xrightarrow{\epsilon}h\circ g^{L}).

The fact that those morphisms are inverse to each other follows from elementary calculations with triangle identities.
∎

Corollary 4.42
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},!}(\mathrm{Corr})
the full flagged subcategory of (the underlying Segal space of)
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathrm{Corr})
on the objects of the form
f_{!}
. Then for all
k\geq 0
we have an isomorphism

\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},!}(\mathrm{Corr})^{\sim}_{k}\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([1],\mathrm{Corr}_{k})^{\sim}.

Proof.

First, it follows from the Segal condition that it would suffice to provide an isomorphism for
k=0
and
k=1
. Observe that for
k=0
both sides are obviously isomorphic to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([1],\mathrm{Cat})^{\sim}
. For
k=1
observe that the objects of
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},!}(\mathrm{Corr})^{\sim}_{1}
are given by diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

in
\mathrm{Corr}
. It follows from the mate correspondence of
lemma

4.41
that the space of such diagrams is isomorphic to the space
of natural transformations
\widetilde{\alpha}:M\rightarrow g^{*}\circ N\circ f_{!}
. A calculation similar to the one in
3.8
shows that

g^{*}\circ N\circ f_{!}\cong\mathscr{C}_{0}\times_{\mathscr{E}_{0}}N\times_{\mathscr{K}_{0}}\mathscr{D}_{0},

so it follows that this data indeed is equivalent to a morphism in
\mathrm{Corr}_{1}
.
∎

5.
Lax functors to Corr

This section is the technical heart of the paper. Our main result is
theorem

5.18
that describes the category of lax functors from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathrm{Corr}
in terms of the category
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
whose description can be found in
5.10
. Our second main result
theorem

5.26
provides an explicit description for a lax colimit of a lax functor
\mathscr{C}\rightsquigarrow\mathrm{Corr}
.

Construction 5.1
.

Denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
the opposite of the category whose objects are intervals
\langle m\rangle
such that every elementary morphism
\{i,i+1\}
is marked with either
0
or
1
and whose morphisms from
\langle m\rangle
to
\langle n\rangle
are given by ordinary morphisms of intervals
([m]\rightarrow[n])
that carry each elementary morphism of
[m]
marked with
0
to a composition of morphisms of
[n]
all of which are marked with
0
. Call a morphism
i:\langle m\rangle\rightarrowtail\langle n\rangle
inert if the underlying morphism of intervals
([m]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[n])
is inert and active if the underlying morphism of intervals is active and in addition only carries morphisms marked with
1
to the composition of morphisms marked with
1
. Call an object elementary if the underlying object of
\mathpzc{Cat}
is elementary. We will denote by
[1]
the elementary segment marked with
0
and by
\langle 1\rangle
the elementary segment marked with 1.

Lemma 5.2
.

The above definition endows
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
with the structure of an algebraic pattern.

Proof.

We need to demonstrate that active and inert morphism indeed form a factorization system on
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
. Let
(\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{f}\langle n\rangle)
be a morphism in
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
, we will provide an active/inert factorization for it. First, let

[m]\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}[l]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[n]

be the usual active/inert factorization of
f
in
\Delta
. Observe that there is a unique way of promoting
[l]
to an object
\langle l\rangle
such that
a
remains an active morphism in
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
, namely we mark an interval in
[l]
with
0
if it is in the image of a
0
-marked interval in
\langle m\rangle
and with
1
if it is in the image of a
1
-marked interval in
\langle m\rangle
. The morphism
i
then automatically lifts to an inert morphism in
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
and it is obvious that this decomposition is unique.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

For
\langle m\rangle\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
denote by
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{mc}}_{\langle m\rangle/}
the full subcategory of
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el}}_{/[n]}
on those inert morphisms
\langle m\rangle\rightarrowtail e
that take edges marked with
i
to edges marked with
i
for
i\in\{0,1\}
, then it is easy to see that
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{mc}}_{\langle m\rangle/}
is coinitial. In other words, a morphism
\mathscr{F}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
is Segal if and only if

\mathscr{F}(\langle m\rangle)\cong\overbrace{\mathscr{F}(e_{1})\times_{\mathscr{F}(1)}\mathscr{F}(e_{2})\times_{\mathscr{F}(2)}...\times_{\mathscr{F}(m-1)}\mathscr{F}(e_{m})}^{\text{$m$ times}},

where
\mathscr{F}(i)
denotes the value of
\mathscr{F}
on the morphism
[0]\xrightarrow{\{i\}}[m]
and
e_{j}
denotes the
j
th elementary edge of
\langle m\rangle
.

Construction 5.4
.

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
the full subcategory of
[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}
containing all objects with the exception of
(0,[0])
. Denote by
i:\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}\hookrightarrow[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}
the natural inclusion and declare a morphism in
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
active (resp. inert) if its image in
[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}
is active (resp. inert) and declare an object elementary if its image under
i
is elementary (here we endow
[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}
with the structure of an algebraic pattern given by
3.4
).

Lemma 5.5
.

\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
is an algebraic pattern such that the category of Segal spaces for it is isomorphic to the category of triples
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D},F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D})
where both
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}
-spaces and
F
is an identity on objects functor.

Proof.

Observe that every morphism in
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
can be decomposed as
(f,\mathrm{id}_{[1]})\circ(\mathrm{id}{Cat},t)
where
t
is the unique nontrivial morphism in
[1]
. Since
t
is considered to be inert, we see that
\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
is indeed an algebraic pattern. It is elementary to see that
i_{*}
restricts to a functor
\mathrm{Seg}_{\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}}(\mathscr{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Seg}_{[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}}(\mathscr{S})
and moreover
i_{*}\mathscr{F}(i(a))\cong\mathscr{F}(a)
for every
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}}(\mathscr{S})
and
a\in\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
and that
i_{*}\mathscr{F}((0,[0]))\cong\mathscr{F}((1,[0]))
. Since
i
is fully faithful, we see that
i_{*}
induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{Seg}_{\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}}(\mathscr{S})
and the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Seg}_{[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}}(\mathscr{S})
on those objects
\mathscr{E}
such that
i_{*}i^{*}\mathscr{E}\cong\mathscr{E}
. Finally, observe that an object
\mathscr{E}
of
\mathrm{Seg}_{[1]\times\mathpzc{Cat}}(\mathscr{S})
can be identified with a pair of Segal spaces
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
together with a functor
F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
and the condition
i_{*}i^{*}\mathscr{E}\cong\mathscr{E}
means precisely that
F
induces an isomorphism on objects.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

The category of Segal spaces for
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
is isomorphic to the category of triples
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D},F:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D})
where both
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}
-spaces and
F
is an isomorphism-on-objects functor.

Proof.

Observe that there is a fully faithful functor
\widetilde{i}:\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
that sends
([n],0)
to the interval of length
n
where every morphism is marked with
0
and sends
([m],1)
to the interval of length
m
where every morphism is marked with
1
. Also observe that

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el}}_{\widetilde{i}(x)/}\cong(\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}})^{\mathrm{el}}_{x/}

for any
x\in\widetilde{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}
, so
\widetilde{i}
is indeed a Segal morphism. Since active morphisms are required to preserve the markings, we also see that
\widetilde{i}
lifts active morphisms uniquely, so by
[
CH19
, Proposition 6.3]

\widetilde{i}_{*}
restricts to a functor between the categories of respective Segal objects and moreover

\widetilde{i}_{*}\mathscr{F}(a)\cong\underset{(a\rightarrowtail\widetilde{i}(b))\in(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\widetilde{i}}}{\lim}\mathscr{F}(b).

Since
\widetilde{i}
is fully faithful, we have
i^{*}i_{*}\cong\mathrm{id}
, so it suffices to prove that
i_{*}i^{*}\cong\mathrm{id}
. For this, first denote by
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{max}}_{/\widetilde{i}}
the full subcategory of
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\widetilde{i}}
on those
(a\rightarrowtail\widetilde{i}(b))
that correspond to the inclusions of maximal ”monochromatic” subintervals, i.e. to inclusions
\langle b\rangle\hookrightarrow\langle a\rangle
such that every element of
\langle b\rangle
is marked with the same number and there is no strictly bigger subinterval of
\langle a\rangle
with this property. Then it is obvious that the inclusion
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{max}}_{/\widetilde{i}}\hookrightarrow(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/\widetilde{i}}
is coinitial. Now it suffices to prove that for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}}(\mathscr{S})
we have

\mathscr{E}(a)\cong\underset{(a\rightarrowtail\widetilde{i}(b))\in(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{max}}_{/\widetilde{i}}}{\lim}\mathscr{E}(b).

Using that
\mathscr{E}
is a Segal space, we can rewrite the right term of the equation as

\underset{(a\rightarrowtail\widetilde{i}(b))\in(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{max}}_{/\widetilde{i}}}{\lim}\;(\underset{(b\rightarrowtail e)\in(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el}}_{b/}}{\lim}\mathscr{E}(e))\cong\underset{(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{max}}_{a/}}{\lim}\mathscr{E}(e)

where
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{max}}_{a/}
denotes the category whose objects are composable pairs
a\rightarrowtail b\rightarrowtail e
where
e
is elementary and
b
is a maximal monochromatic subinterval of
a
. Finally, observe that
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el},\mathrm{max}}_{a/}\cong(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{el}}_{a/}
since maximal monochromatic subintervals cover
\langle a\rangle
and only intersect along boundary points.
∎

Construction 5.7
.

Given a Segal space
\mathscr{C}
, denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}
the following presheaf on
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
: for
\langle n\rangle\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
let
\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}(\langle n\rangle)
be the space of morphisms
f:[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
in
\mathrm{Cat}
that send edges marked with
0
to identity morphisms. It is easy to see that, if
g:\langle m\rangle\rightarrow\langle n\rangle
is a morphism in
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
, then
f\circ g
also satisfies the conditions to be an element of
\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}(\langle m\rangle)
.

Notation 5.8
.

Denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{nun}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
the full subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
on those morphisms
[n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
that take all non-identity morphisms in
[n]
to non-identity morphisms in
\mathscr{C}
. Observe that the natural inclusion
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}^{\mathrm{nun}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}_{/\mathscr{C}}
is cofinal since every
[n]\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{C}
uniquely factors as
[n]\xrightarrow{s}[m]\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}}\mathscr{C}
, where
\widetilde{f}\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{nun}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
and
s
is a surjective morphism.

Proposition 5.9
.

The
\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}
is a Segal space, so in particular its total space obtains a structure of an algebraic pattern, which we will denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
. Moreover, we have

\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{nun}}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\lim}\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}
is a Segal space follows from the isomorphism

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}([n],\mathscr{C})\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrowtail e)\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{el}}_{[n]/}}{\lim}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(e,\mathscr{C})

together with the observation that if
(\langle n\rangle\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{C})
satisfies the condition of
5.7
, then so do all the elementary subintervals
\langle 1\rangle\hookrightarrow\langle n\rangle
of
\langle n\rangle
.

To prove the second claim, it would suffice to prove that

\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}\cong\underset{([n]\rightarrow\mathscr{C})\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{nun}}_{/\mathscr{C}}}{\mathrm{colim}}[n]^{\rightarrow}

as presheaves on
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}}
since then we can use the same argument as in
2.2
. To prove this, first observe that both
\mathscr{C}^{\rightarrow}
and each of the
[n]^{\rightarrow}
are colimits of their Yoneda cocones in
\mathscr{P}((\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow})^{\mathrm{op}})
. Denote by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}},\mathrm{nun}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
the category whose objects are morphisms
\langle n\rangle\rightarrow[m]\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, where the first morphism belongs to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[m]^{\rightarrow}}
and the second to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{nun}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
, and the morphisms are diagrams

Proposition 5.10
.

\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}}(\mathscr{S})
is the category of triples

(F:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{C},G:\mathscr{D}:\rightarrow\mathscr{C},H:\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}),

where
H
is a functor over
\mathscr{C}
that induces an isomorphism on the space of objects and also an isomorphism
(\mathscr{E}_{f})^{\sim}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}(\mathscr{D}_{f})^{\sim}
for any non-identity morphism
f
in
\mathscr{C}
(where
(\mathscr{D}_{f})^{\sim}
and
(\mathscr{E}_{f})^{\sim}
denote the spaces of morphisms lying over
f
).

Proof.

First, observe that there is a fully faithful inclusion
i_{\mathscr{C}}:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}_{/\mathscr{C}}
, where in the codomain we view
\mathscr{C}
as a Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
-space corresponding to the triple
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C},\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{C}})
using the notation of
lemma

5.5
. Using
2.1
we see that the Segal
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}_{/\mathscr{C}}
-spaces can be identified with triples
(F,G,H)
as in the statement of the proposition, except
H
is only required to be an isomorphism on objects. Observe that since active morphisms preserve markings,
i_{\mathscr{C}}
admits unique lifting of active morphisms, so by
[
CH19
, Proposition 6.3.]

i_{\mathscr{C},*}
restricts to a functor between the categories of Segal spaces. Since
i
is an inclusion of a full subcategory, we see that
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
can be identified with those Segal spaces
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}_{/\mathscr{C}}}(\mathscr{S})
for which
\mathscr{F}\cong i_{\mathscr{C},*}i^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{F}
. It is easy to see that
i_{\mathscr{C},*}i^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{F}(e)\cong\mathscr{F}(e)
for elementary objects
e
of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}_{/\mathscr{C}}
of the form
([0]\xrightarrow{\{c\}}\mathscr{C})
for
c\in\mathscr{C}
,
(\langle 1\rangle\xrightarrow{g}\mathscr{C})
for all morphisms
g
in
\mathscr{C}
and
([1]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}_{c^{\prime}}}\mathscr{C})
for all
c^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, but

i_{\mathscr{C},*}i^{*}_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{F}([1]\xrightarrow{f}\mathscr{C})\cong\mathscr{F}(\langle 1\rangle\xrightarrow{\overline{f}}\mathscr{C}),

where
f
sends an edge marked with
0
to a non-identity morphism and
\overline{f}
has the same underlying morphism but for which the edge is marked with 1. In other words, the full subcategory in question consists of those
\mathscr{F}
that have the same value on non-identity morphisms
\langle 1\rangle\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
regardless of the marking, which concludes the proof.
∎

Notation 5.11
.

Given
\langle m\rangle\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\rightarrow}
, denote by
[\widetilde{m}]
the interval obtained from
[m]
by contracting all edges marked with
0
. If we are additionally given
(\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{f}[n])\in\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}
, we see that, since those edges are all sent to identity morphisms,
f
gives rise to
\widetilde{f}:[\widetilde{m}]\rightarrow[n]
that sends all edges in
[\widetilde{m}]
to the image in
[n]
of their unique preimage in
\langle m\rangle
. Similarly, every morphism
\langle m\rangle\rightarrow\langle l\rangle
induces a morphism
[\widetilde{m}]\rightarrow[\widetilde{l}]
, and so we obtain a functor
U:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}
. Denote by

(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{int}}(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})\times_{\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}}\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}

the category whose objects are pairs
(f,g\rightarrowtail U(f))
and morphisms are given by commutative diagrams of the form

Denote by
p:(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}
the natural projection.

Proposition 5.12
.

The fiber of
p
over
g:[k]\rightarrow[n]
is isomorphic to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}
and moreover for any morphism
h:[k]\rightarrow[s]
fitting in the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[s]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

the fiber

(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]})_{h}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}\times_{\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}}[1],

when viewed as a correspondence from
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]})_{[s]}
to
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]})_{[k]}
, is isomorphic to
h^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}
in the notation of
remark

2.12
.

Proof.

Observe that if we view
f:\langle m\rangle\rightarrow[n]
as an ordinary morphism in
\mathrm{Cat}
, then it factors as
([m]\xrightarrow{s_{f}}[\widetilde{m}]\xrightarrow{\widetilde{f}}[n])
, where
s_{f}
sends all morphisms marked with
0
to identity morphisms in
[\widetilde{m}]
. It is immediate from the definition that the fiber of
p
over
g
has pairs
(\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{f}[n],[\widetilde{m}]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[k])
where
i
is an inert morphism over
[n]
in
\Delta
and
g\circ i\cong\widetilde{f}
as objects and a morphism from
(\langle l\rangle\xrightarrow{t}[n],[\widetilde{l}]\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}[k])
to
(\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{f}[n],[\widetilde{m}]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[k])
given by a diagram in
\Delta
of the form

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
u
is a morphism in
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}
. Observe that morphisms
i\circ s_{f}
and
j\circ s_{t}
in the diagram above can be viewed as elements of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}
, and a morphism
u
can also be viewed as a morphism in this category, so this defines a functor
F:p_{g}\rightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}
.

Conversely, given a cellular morphism
c:[m]\rightarrow[k]
we can first decompose it as
([m]\xrightarrow{s_{c}}[\widetilde{m}]\overset{i_{c}}{\rightarrowtail}[k])
, where
s_{c}
is surjective and
i_{c}
is inert. Now we mark an edge of
[m]
with
1
if its image under
c
is a nontrivial edge in
[k]
and with
0
if
c
sends it to an identity morphism. It is then easy to see that with these markings the morphism
g\circ c:\langle m\rangle\rightarrow[n]
can be viewed as an element of
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}
and the pair
(g\circ c:\langle m\rangle\rightarrow[n],[\widetilde{m}]\overset{i_{c}}{\rightarrowtail}[k])
as an element of
p_{g}
. Moreover, since edges marked with
0
are precisely those that are sent to identity by
c
, we see that every morphism
h:[m]\rightarrow[l]
over
[k]
can also be uniquely lifted to a morphism in
p_{g}
, so this construction indeed defines a functor
G:\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}\rightarrow p_{g}
and it is easy to see that
F
and
G
are mutually inverse.

To prove the second claim, first let
(\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{f}[n],[\widetilde{m}]\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}[k])\in p_{g}
and
(\langle o\rangle\xrightarrow{q}[n],[\widetilde{o}]\overset{j}{\rightarrowtail}[s])\in p_{v}
, then the correspondence in question assigns to them the space of morphisms
b
making the following diagram commute

\overline{b}
as above uniquely lifts to

making the other diagram commute, thus providing an isomorphism between the two correspondences.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}
can be viewed as a full subcategory of
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
on objects of the form
(f,\mathrm{id}_{U(f)})
. Using this fact, we can view
\mathscr{P}((\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{op}})
as a full subcategory of
\mathscr{P}(((\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U})^{\mathrm{op}})
on those
\mathscr{F}
for which
\mathscr{F}(f,g\rightarrowtail U(f))\cong\mathscr{F}(f,\mathrm{id}_{U(f)})
. This condition is equivalent to requiring that for an inert morphism
i:[k^{\prime}]\hookrightarrow[k]
we have
\mathscr{F}|_{p_{[k^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{g\circ i}[n]}}\cong i^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}\mathscr{F}|_{p_{[k]\xrightarrow{g}[n]}}
. Indeed, if this condition holds then we get

\mathscr{F}(f,g\rightarrowtail U(f))\cong\mathscr{F}(f,\mathrm{id}_{U(f)})

by applying it to the inert morphism
g\rightarrowtail U(f)
. Conversely, if we assume
\mathscr{F}(f,g\rightarrowtail U(f))\cong\mathscr{F}(f,\mathrm{id}_{U(f)})
, then given
i:h\rightarrowtail g
and an object of
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
of the form
(f,h\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}g\rightarrowtail U(f))
we see that

\mathscr{F}(f,h\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}g\rightarrowtail U(f))\cong\mathscr{F}(f,g\rightarrowtail U(f)),

which is exactly the condition
\mathscr{F}|_{p_{[k^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{g\circ i}[n]}}\cong i^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}\mathscr{F}|_{p_{[k]\xrightarrow{g}[n]}}
.

.

the full subcategory of

on objects of the form

and by
\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}})}_{/[n]}
the category with objects given by
\langle m\rangle\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}[k]\xrightarrow{f}[n]
, where
a
takes edges marked with
0
to identity, and morphisms are given by diagrams

We will also denote by
\overline{i}:\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]}
its restriction to
\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}
.

Lemma 5.15
.

If
([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])
represents an object of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]}
, then we have a sequence of isomorphisms

\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}\cong p_{([k]\xrightarrow{t\circ b}[n])}\cong\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}.

Moreover, for any morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[s]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[s^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

in
\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]}
we have that the space of morphisms in
(\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
lying over it, viewed as a correspondence from
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k^{\prime}]}
to
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}
, is isomorphic to
w^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that to prove the first claim it suffices to prove the isomorphism

\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}\cong p_{([k]\xrightarrow{t\circ b}[n])}

since the second isomorphism would then follow from
5.12
. Observe that objects of
\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}
are given by diagrams of the form

\hbox to267.92pt{\vbox to88.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 133.95729pt\lower-43.97923pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Observe that, since active and inert morphisms form a factorization system in
\Delta
, the object
[l]
and morphisms
j
and
a
are uniquely determined by
i:[\widetilde{m}]\rightarrowtail[k]
; in other words we have an isomorphism between the objects of
\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}
and
p_{([k]\xrightarrow{t\circ b}[n])}
. A morphism in
\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}
is given by a diagram of the form

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The only morphism in this diagram that is not obviously determined by
h
is
g:[l]\rightarrow[q]
, so to provide the isomorphism between morphisms in
\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}
and
p_{([k]\xrightarrow{t\circ b}[n])}
it suffices to prove that it is uniquely defined by
h
. To do so, observe that since
j\circ U(h)\cong i
we have that
U(h)
is inert (otherwise it would contradict the uniqueness of the active/inert factorization). Similarly, since
\widetilde{j}\circ g\cong\widetilde{i}
we see that
g
is also an inert morphism, and so it follows that it must be the inert part of the active/inert decomposition of
c\circ U(h)
.

To prove the final claim simply observe that the space of morphisms in question is obviously isomorphic to the space of morphisms lying over

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[n]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\circ b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}\circ b^{\prime}}

in
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
and this is isomorphic to
w^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}
by
5.12
.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

i:(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}\hookrightarrow(\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
identifies
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})
with the full subcategory of
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})
such that for any object
([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])
we have that

\mathscr{F}|_{\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}}\cong b^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}\mathscr{F}|_{\widehat{p}_{i([s]=\joinrel=[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}}.

Proof.

Since
i
is fully faithful it induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})
and the full subcategory of
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})
for which
\mathscr{F}\cong i_{!}i^{*}\mathscr{F}
, so to prove the claim it suffices to calculate
i_{!}
. Fix an object
x\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=}([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n],[\widetilde{m}]\rightarrowtail[k])
, then by definition

i_{!}i^{*}\mathscr{F}(x)\cong\underset{(i(y)\rightarrow x)\in(i/(\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U})}{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{F}(y).

Observe that any morphism to
([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])
from the object in the image of
\overline{i}
has the form

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Observe that the bottom morphism lies in the image of
\overline{i}
, so this gives a decomposition
\widehat{h}\cong\widehat{b}\circ i(h)
in
\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]}
. Moreover, by
lemma

5.15
the correspondence of morphisms in
(i/(\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
lying over
\widehat{h}
is isomorphic to
(h\circ b)^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}
, and this in turn is isomorphic to
h^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}\circ b^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}
. This implies that for a given
(i(y)\xrightarrow{f}x)
the category of decompositions of the form
(i(y)\xrightarrow{i(\widetilde{f})}z\xrightarrow{a}x)
where
a
belongs to
(i/(\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}})^{\mathrm{id}}
is contractible, proving that this category is indeed cofinal.

Finally, if we represent
x
by a cellular morphism
(\langle m\rangle\xrightarrow{c_{m}}[k])
, then it is easy to see that
(i/(\mathpzc{Cat}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int},\mathrm{id}}
is a category of commutative diagrams

where
c_{l}
is a cellular morphism representing an object in
\widehat{p}_{[s]=\joinrel=[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n]}\cong\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[s]}
, and so the claim follows by
remark

2.12
.
∎

Remark 5.17
.

We can view
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}}(\mathscr{S})
as a full subcategory of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]}^{(\mathrm{act},\rightarrow)})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})
through a composition of full embeddings

\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}}(\mathscr{S})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathpzc{Cat}^{{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}}_{/[n]},\mathscr{S})\overset{j_{!}}{\hookrightarrow}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})\overset{i_{!}}{\hookrightarrow}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Cat}}((\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act},\rightarrow}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U},\mathscr{S})

where
j
denotes the inclusion of
remark

5.13
. Observe that in this case the restriction of
\mathscr{F}\in\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}}(\mathscr{S})
to any
\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}\cong\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}
is an object of
\mathrm{Corr}_{k}
. Indeed, by
lemma

5.16
we have

\mathscr{F}|_{\widehat{p}_{([k]\overset{b}{\twoheadrightarrow}[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}}\cong b^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}\mathscr{F}|_{\widehat{p}_{i([s]=\joinrel=[s]\xrightarrow{t}[n])}},

and so, since
b^{*}_{\mathrm{Cell}}
takes
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[s]}}(\mathscr{S})
to
\mathrm{Seg}_{\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{Cell}}_{/[k]}}(\mathscr{S})
, we are reduced to proving the claim for the restriction of
\mathscr{F}
to
(\mathpzc{Cat}_{/[n]})^{\mathrm{int}}_{/U}
. Similarly, we can use
remark

5.13
to reduce further to proving the statement for
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/[n]}
, in which case it follows from the Segal condition for
\mathscr{F}
described in
remark

5.3
.

Theorem 5.18
.

For a given Segal space
\mathscr{C}
denote by
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax},!}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Corr})
the flagged subcategory of (the underlying Segal space of)
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax}}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Corr})
having the same objects, but only those morphisms
L^{\mathrm{lax}}\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax}}(\mathrm{Corr})
that factor through
\mathrm{Ar}^{\mathrm{lax},!}(\mathrm{Corr})
in the notation of
4.42
. Then there is an equivalence of categories between
\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{lax},!}_{\mathrm{Cat}}(\mathscr{C},\mathrm{Corr})
and
\mathrm{Cat}^{\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}}_{/\mathscr{C}}
.

Proof.

First, observe that by
4.34
and
5.9
it suffices to prove the theorem for
\mathscr{C}\cong[n]
. We will first provide an isomorphism between the underling groupoids of both categories. Observe that the category
(L^{\mathrm{lax}}[n])_{m}
is by definition isomorphic to the subcategory of
\mathpzc{Cat}^{\mathrm{act},\rightarrow}_{/[n]}
containing objects of the form
([m]\overset{a}{\twoheadrightarrow}[l]\xrightarrow{f}[n])
and morphisms given by diagrams
